# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

## Climate change is expected to bring Tonga various impacts that include more heavy rainfall events, severe tropical cyclones and increased sea level events, which will require adaptation for people, infrastructure, and coastal ecosystems**. Tonga faces a prolonged long-term threat from permanent land inundation and wave-driven flooding**, and some studies have suggested that significant flood risks exist as well as permanent loss from sea-level rise (SLR) in the absence of future adaptation measures. The most vulnerable areas are located on low-lying islands, such as Vava’u, Ha’apai, Tongatapu, and other outlying islands to Tongatapu. On Tongatapu there is clear evidence of the impacts of local sea-level rise and the long-term flooding associated with SLR. The most vulnerable areas are located on low-lying islands, such as Vava’u, Ha’apai, Tongatapu, and other outlying islands to Tongatapu. On Tongatapu there is clear evidence of the impacts of local sea-level rise and the long-term flooding associated with SLR.

## Output 1 Strengthened knowledge, capacity and engagement for incorporating climate risks into long-term adaptation planning supported through multi-sector, multi-stakeholder engagement and dialogue platforms. This output will be achieved by: (i) strengthening the national capacity to collect and analyze data and information on climate risk; (ii) strengthening the national capacity to develop and implement climate-resilient development plans and strategies that incorporate climate risk into national planning processes; and (iii) building the capacity of local government, village committees and NGOs to participate in climate risk-informed plans.

## Output 2 Increased local knowledge and local actions for effective implementation and assessment of climate risks. G1.1 strengthens the mechanisms for collecting and analyzing data and information for better informed climate risk management at national level. G1.2 strengthens the capacity of Tonga’s communities to collect, analyze and use data on adaptation strategies for long-term resilient planning and transformative adaptation for Tonga based on climate risks and regional context.

## Output 3 Reduced vulnerabilities of coastal communities in Ha’apai to climate hazards through coastal protection measures. This output will be achieved by: (i) strengthening the capacity of local government, village committees and NGOs to participate in climate risk-informed plans; and (ii) building the capacity of local government, village committees and NGOs to participate in climate risk-informed planning, and providing technical support for implementation.

## Project 4 The project proposes to directly benefit 3608 people, or 0.8% of the population. Output 1 supports building the capacity of Tonga’s communities to collect, analyze and use data on adaptation strategies for long-term resilient planning and transformative adaptation for Tonga based on climate risks and regional context. Output 2 strengthens the mechanisms for collecting and analyzing data and information for better informed climate risk management at national level. Output 3 reduces vulnerabilities of coastal communities in Ha’apai to climate hazards through coastal protection measures.

### 5. Indirect beneficiaries are the 96,571 remaining total population of Tonga (Tonga's population is 100,179 people as per 2003 census). The indirect benefits will be achieved through the development and implementation of the island spatial plans developed, and the outcomes developed through the multistakeholder engagement platform. As the first to be updated are the village and district level participatory risk informed plans, and under (Activity 1.3) building the capacity of local government officials, community members, and other stakeholders in Tonga to participate effectively in planning processes and informing future COPs (guiding the other 7 island Divisions). In addition to the Strategic Development Plan for Tonga, the proposed project will also contribute towards the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy. The plans of development and spatial planning (community development plan, district plans and island strategic plans) to consider climate change impacts and vulnerability are being developed in consultation with communities. This includes workshops/consultations for developing village and district spatial plans for all 5 islands and 1 land-level geographical region planned for the project. These consultations will be held during the period of 2017-2018. The workshops will include consultations determining adaptation needs, gender needs, participatory vulnerability assessment and planning. 7. Geographical setting: The Kingdom of Tonga spreads across more than 100 small islands, many of which are uninhabited. The total land area is approximately 698 km² (270 sq mi), with a coastline of about 453 km (281 miles). The main island of Tongatapu covers an area of 260 km² (100 sq mi) and land that spreads across an estimated combined land and sea area of 270,000 km². Of the 178 lands, 36 are inhabited by a total population of approximately 105,940 people as at March 2020 (Tongatapu: 100,000; Ha'apai: 10,000; Vava'u: 7,800). The Pacific tectonic plates and land within the Ring of Fire where intense seismic activities occur. The islands are formed in the form of a ring around the Tonga Trench, which is one of the deepest parts of the world's oceans (10,542 m or 34,589 ft). The islands can be divided into four main island groups: the Tongatapu group in the south (population 74,320), the Ha'apai group in the middle (population 16,000) and Vava'u group in the north (population 15,800). The largest of these islands is Tongatapu. Tonga lies between 19°S to 24°S latitude and 173°E to 178°E longitude.

Tonga's population is the most populous island with the highest population density. Of Tonga's total population of 105,979 people, 60% live on Tongatapu (population: 64,200). The second largest island group is Ha'apai which has a population of approximately 13,800 and the third largest is Vava'u with a population of about 15,700. The islands of Tonga are located in the greater Nacula area (Tonga). The island of Tongatapu has the largest land area of Tonga's islands (approximately 270km²), followed by Ha'apai and Vava'u.

The main economic activities on the islands include agriculture, fishing, tourism, and small-scale industries. The majority of the islands are located in a highly productive land with an upper layer of volcanic soils derived from ancient volcanic ash falls.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

### Background

Developing a “Fragile” Area from fast-rising climate and non-climate related disaster events, climate induced slow onset stress processes the coastal erosion, ocean acidification and other forms of environmental degradation in the medium term will drive both migration and displacement.

In extreme cases, destruction of habitat and agricultural viability reduces the prospects for return to the home environment and increases vulnerability. The Tongan population is highly mobile with 10% living outside the country (2015). Migration and resettlement is further complicated by land ownership complexities in Tonga whereby under the Constitution of Tonga (the Constitution), land can be owned only by a male member of the royal family, but not by women. This has led to many Tongan families, but only to the eldest “legitimate” son, and not to “illegitimate” elder sons or to women or younger male heirs. Notably, this is in contrast with other Pacific Island countries where land ownership is more equitable.

### Risk Assessment

The Tonga Islands are ranked as one of the most vulnerable countries in the world for climate change impacts. The country ranks 8th most vulnerable and 5th most exposed out of 169 (MIP 2014). The ND-GAIN index score (out of 100) which summarizes a country’s capacity to adapt to climate change, is 37. Tonga has the lowest score on the ND-GAIN index as it is 41.8 and its overall rank is 142 out of 169*. Tonga's population already lives in a highly vulnerable environment with limited resources and resilience. The country’s vulnerability to climate change is further exacerbated by inter-island systems, saline intrusion, wave-driven flooding, and permanent inundation.

### Climate Change

Tonga’s climate is impacted by a wet season usually from November to April that has both moderate and variable rainfall, and dry season from May to October. Mean annual precipitation averages 1,666 millimeters (mm) over the period of record (1951–2014). The wet season is characterized by frequent tropical cyclones, which are associated with high wind speeds and heavy rainfall that migrates across the Pacific south of the equator. Year-to-year variability occurs under the influence of the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) phenomenon.

### Migration

The Tonga Islands are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their low elevation and limited resources, in particular the depletion of potable water, and tropical cyclones that occur during the wet season, causing extensive damage to local communities. The 2018/2019 El Niño event was particularly severe with a peak sea level rise of approximately 54 millimeters (mm) on average between October 2017 and March 2018. This is in contrast with the 36 mm recorded during the La Niña event in 2015/2016.

### Conclusion

For western South-Pacific islands including Tonga, climate change is expected to bring more heavy rainfall events and severe tropical cyclones and severe sea level events, which will require adaptation for people, infrastructure and coastal ecosystems.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Infrastructure and Settlements Key Findings for the Pacific from the United Nations Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Fifth Assessment Report (AR5): Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability: Pacific Islands Framework: Infrastructure and Settlements (Australian National University [ANU] Climate Change Communication and Information Programme, 2013)

### Key Findings

* **I.** The projected increase in sea level is the most significant climate change impact on infrastructure and settlements.
* **II.** The rate of sea-level rise has increased since the mid-20th century (since 1950) and is projected to continue increasing at a faster rate than during the past several decades, with an average global increase in sea level of about 3 mm per year over this period.
* **III.** The projected increase in sea level is the most significant climate change impact on infrastructure and settlements.

Monthly sea levels at Nuku'alofa, Tonga from 1993-2021 shows an increase of 6.8mm / yr and NASA's sea level projection tool output for Tongatapu SSP7/2.0 shows a potential mitigation rise of 2.77m by the 21st century. This extent of sea level rise will have catastrophic impacts on low-lying coastal areas, including the main island of Tongatapu and the Kolonga's of the Ha'apai Peninsula, much of which is already subject to damaging wave impacts and in extremely low-lying.

### Surface air temperature and sea-surface temperatures are projected to continue to increase over the course of the 21st century. The rate of warming will be greater than that experienced during the past several decades. Under a high emissions pathway (RCP8.5), warming of around 3.6°C is projected by the end of the century for Tongatapu and Nuku'alofa. This warming will have significant impacts on sea level rise, coral reefs, and the defences that reef ecosystems play in protecting and maintaining soft shore environments throughout Tonga. Climate change stress in the marine environment is expected to be particularly high in the future. Of concern are even small increases in ocean surface temperature which can lead to bleaching of corals and other organisms.

### Rainfall
Due to the spatial distribution of the islands of the country, there is a disparity in the nature of rainfall in the country. Tonga's three main islands (Tongatapu, Vava'u and Ha'apai) have an average annual rainfall between 1971-2007. Tongatapu reported an average of 1,721 mm, Vava'u an average of 2,150 mm, and Ha'apai an average of 1,836 mm.

### Storms and Tropical cyclones
Storms are by far the most common marine hazards affecting Tonga as indicated below.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Figure 2 Hazards by occurrence during 1980-2020

Tropical cyclones have huge impacts in Tonga, where a cyclone with a 100-year return period (50% chance of occurring within the current generation) is likely to occur every 3 years. In the past decade, there has been an increase in the number and intensity of tropical storms, which have increased over the last 4 decades **[6]**. Variability between ocean basins, and particularly the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO), can influence the frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones in Tonga. The ENSO index has been at or above +1 since crossing the Tongan archipelago between the years 1969 - 2011 with an average of 5.3, which is a record high for this region. This has resulted in 4 Category 4 and 5 storms. These findings are consistent with long-term global projections globally that indicate that the number of more intense-category storms may increase. The recent record in Tonga was set by Cyclone Gita (2018) which had a wind speed of 300 km/h, crossing the Tongan archipelago between the years 1969 - 2011 with an average of 5.3, which is a record high for this region.

## General characteristics change engines and climatic hazards

Relatively also, the islands of Tonga are associated with the Tonga subduction zone and one of the fastest moving and most seismically active plate boundaries in the world. The region is an area of intense seismic activity. Tonga has several active sites of volcanic activity, including the island of Vava'u. Individual and island groups have been subject to tectonic uplift, subsidence, and erosion over geological time.

Tsunami modeling indicates, however, suggests that Tonga's vulnerability to tsunami events is likely to be greatest along the Tonga Trench. Tsunami modeling undertaken in 2011 (see Figure 4) predicted a tsunami with a maximum run-up of +8 m at Manua and Kolonga. This tsunami was also likely to impact the northern coast areas of Ha'apai, Vava'u, and Tongatapu.

Tonga is also vulnerable to tsunamis generated by earthquakes along the Hikurangi subduction zone off the east coast of New Zealand and the north coast. These predicted impacts according to modeling undertaken in 2011 (see Figure 4) are likely to be generated of either +1 m, +3 m, +6m and +8m. The maximum tsunami run-up is predicted at Manua and Kolonga with an overall flow depth on the right bank of ±7 m magnitude earthquakes.

Tonga has also experienced tsunamis in 2009 and 2012. Violent eruption of the Hunga Tonga-Hunga Ha'apai submarine volcano in January 2014 generated a tsunami with run-up heights up to 65m struck the uninhabited island of Tofua in Tonga, and a 1.2km tsunami struck Nuku'alofa and Nukuleka on Tongatapu.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

## The Tonga tsunami that followed the eruption affected 95 per cent of the Tongan population directly, causing widespread damage to houses, schools, roads and power water supply networks [2] and setting a precedent of relocation for many families. The total economic loss was estimated at US$30 million (equivalent to US$186 million or approximately 421 million Tongan Pa‘anga), or more than 36 per cent of Tonga’s GDP [3]. According to the World Bank, the tsunami caused a significant increase in poverty and food insecurity. The disaster also had an impact on health care: food security; and caused loss of assets, where Tongans and poor and most vulnerable families were hit the hardest. Overall, 102 people died as a result of the eruption and tsunami [4]. According to the World Bank’s Human Development Report (HDR) [5], the disaster affected 37 per cent of households in Tonga. The United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (UNOCHA) reported that 1,026 people were killed by the eruption and tsunami; 489 people were injured; and 10,000 people were officially recorded injured [6]. The tsunami also displaced coastal communities throughout Tongatapu, Eua and the Ha‘apai Islands. According to UNOCHA, 352 houses were destroyed in Tongatapu, 47 on Eua Island and 81 on the Ha‘apai Islands following the event [7]. The tsunami waves largely affected the western coastal areas of Tongatapu and Ha‘apai divisions, with Manono Island being completely devastated. According to UNOCHA, 250 houses were destroyed in Tongatapu; 47 houses on Eua Island; and less affected in Ha‘apai [8]. Overall, 250 houses were damaged and 3125 people were displaced by the impacts of the eruption and tsunami. According to UNOCHA, 69 per cent of households in Tongatapu were affected by the disaster, followed by Eua (73%), Ha‘apai (48%) and Manono Island (30%). The majority of those affected were women and children [9]. The most severely affected areas were the coastal villages in Tongatapu and Ha‘apai divisions immediately following the disaster. These houses were based in low-lying coastal areas, which are vulnerable to flooding during high tides.

### Climate-sensitive economy: World Bank data shows that Tonga has one of the world’s smallest economies, as measured by GDP per capita (US$1085), and is ranked 23rd out of 24 Pacific Island countries. The country has a population of just over 96,493 people in service. In Tonga, the number of workers participating in the seasonal schemes in Australia and New Zealand has increased significantly since 2017. However, the majority of these workers are not Tongan nationals but rather foreign workers hired there [10]. However, remittances from families overseas (most in Australia and New Zealand) have been the largest contributor to Tonga’s Gross National Product and a major source of its foreign exchange each year [11].

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 12

## Background

### Remittances and Economic Growth

Remittances have been a critical source of income for Tonga's economy, accounting for approximately 37% of GDP in 2020, the highest in the world [1], but which increased to 48.6% of GDP in 2021 [2]. Remittances have also been critical in strengthening resilience at the household, community and national levels and potentially contributing an additional 3-5 percentage points to Tonga's economic growth.

### Climate Change and Adaptation

Climate change is a major challenge for Tonga. The country has experienced significant increases in sea surface temperatures, increased frequency of extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones, and rising sea levels. These changes have had adverse impacts on the environment, economy, and social well-being of Tongans [3]. Despite its small size, Tonga is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its low-lying coastal areas and reliance on agriculture.

### Economic Sectoral Impacts

In 2021, aside from the contribution from remittances which were 46.2% of GDP in 2021 [3], Tonga's economy is highly dependent on remittances (48.6%) and agriculture (39.5%). Remittances are a critical source of income for Tongans, accounting for approximately 37% of GDP in 2020, the highest in the world [1]. However, the contribution from remittances has increased to 48.6% of GDP in 2021 [2].

### Agriculture and Climate Change

In 2022, agriculture contributed to 19.35% of GDP while industry contributed 10.44%, and services made up 60.6%. Significant climate change impacts are expected on the agricultural sector which is highly vulnerable due to its reliance on rain-fed agriculture, limited irrigation infrastructure, and lack of access to modern equipment for agriculture, as well as buildings and other infrastructure.

### Adaptation Strategies

The sectoral adaptation strategies aim at reducing vulnerability in the agricultural sector. The strategies include:

- **Agriculture:** Strengthening resilience through improved water management practices, diversification of crops, and increased use of climate-smart technologies.
- **Fisheries:** Improving fishery management practices to ensure sustainable fishing and reduce overfishing.

### Long-Term Transformative Adaptation

The project is designed to address underlying barriers to long-term adaptation and specifically, undertake an approach leading to transformative adaptation. Ongoing climate change risks, their impacts on the environment, economy, and social well-being of Tongans are significant. The project will work towards gaining inter-institutional and civil society support for, as well as being led by a multi-stakeholder engagement platform that will draw on knowledge and experience from across the country.

## Supporting climate risk informed decision making.

Adaptive capacity gaps also remain within limited capacities within the public sector that may affect agenda setting, and the ability to anticipate, guide, and make decisions for long-term adaptation planning. These gaps are particularly acute in small island developing states (SIDS) where there is a lack of institutional capacity to address unique attributes of different islands, especially Tongatapu (such as changeability of reef island, maintenance of sediment supply, etc.). The project will support the Government in addressing these gaps by building adaptive capacity and providing guidance on the various risks such as future sea level rise and storm surge under different climate scenarios and return periods, which extends to the year 2100.

### The needs for strengthening knowledge, capacity and engagement for incorporating climate risks into long-term adaptation planning

The project will support the Government in addressing these gaps by building adaptive capacity and providing guidance on the various risks such as future sea level rise and storm surge under different climate scenarios and return periods, which extends to the year 2100. The project will determine the approach for how local governments, village committees, and NGOs can be supported to integrate climate change adaptation into their planning processes. This will include a review of existing policies and strategies that are in place at national level as well as at the local government level.

### Building adaptive capacity

The project will support the Government in addressing these gaps by building adaptive capacity and providing guidance on the various risks such as future sea level rise and storm surge under different climate scenarios and return periods, which extends to the year 2100. The project will determine the approach for how local governments, village committees, and NGOs can be supported to integrate climate change adaptation into their planning processes. This will include a review of existing policies and strategies that are in place at national level as well as at the local government level.

### Supporting climate risk information analysis and use

The aim of this consultation data and analytical expertise is to provide the Government with an overview of the current state of knowledge on climate change risks, vulnerability and adaptation. The project will support the Government in addressing these gaps by building adaptive capacity and providing guidance on the various risks such as future sea level rise and storm surge under different climate scenarios and return periods, which extends to the year 2100.

### Capacity Building Strategy and Plan

The Capacity Building Strategy and Plan will be an overarching Government owned strategy to address the needs and gaps identified in the project. The Capacity Building Strategy and Plan will provide a framework for the implementation of the overall approach for capacity building undertaken within all three components of the project. The Capacity Building Strategy and Plan will also identify key areas where further support is required from the Green Climate Fund, including through co-financing with other donors and Government initiatives that involve capacity building and training to ensure there is no overlap/replication and will also provide a basis for the development of a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) in line with the Paris Agreement. The project will assist the Government to take forward beyond the lifetime of the project in ensuring its capacities for resilience building in the country are sustainable, including through capacity building and training. This will be achieved by:

1. Developing a national climate change adaptation strategy and plan.
2. Producing maps and knowledge on material based coastal risk assessment and incorporating these decisions for long-term planning.
3. Providing technical assistance to the Government in developing a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) in line with the Paris Agreement.

The project will support the Government in addressing these gaps by building adaptive capacity and providing guidance on the various risks such as future sea level rise and storm surge under different climate scenarios and return periods, which extends to the year 2100. The project will determine the approach for how local governments, village committees, and NGOs can be supported to integrate climate change adaptation into their planning processes. This will include a review of existing policies and strategies that are in place at national level as well as at the local government level.

The Government staff will be provided with a package of training and policy briefs relevant to the specific sector for which they are responsible.

equipment and tools can be fully utilized for use in generating climate and risk information and data such as satellite imagery, aerial photography, drone imagery, LiDAR topography and bathymetry. Training/mentoring and training materials will be provided to ensure Tonga has the capacity to utilize these tools effectively. The project will also provide technical assistance, supporting the development planning and decision-making. This will build Tonga's technical capacity in climate risk data collection, analysis and interpretation. It is expected that this will lead to more informed decision making by Tonga.

The project will focus on coastal vulnerability assessment and adaptation planning for Tongatapu Island. The project will be prioritising high-risk areas by strengthening national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of coastal risks, with a particular emphasis on the most vulnerable communities. This will include the development of an early warning system to alert communities in advance of extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones and storm surges.

The Tongatapu Island is located in the South Pacific Ocean. The island has a total land area of 268 km², with a population of approximately 105,000 people (as at 2017). It is one of the most densely populated islands in the world and is home to the capital city Nuku'alofa.

The project will focus on coastal vulnerability assessment and adaptation planning for Tongatapu Island. The project will be prioritising high-risk areas by strengthening national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of coastal risks, with a particular emphasis on the most vulnerable communities. This will include the development of an early warning system to alert communities in advance of extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones and storm surges.

## Detail of the extremely low topography of this blue zone adjacent to Manuku Village and the proposed site of coastal protective measures shows that much of the land is less than 1m above mean sea level. Any major breach of the unprotected shoreline in this area would result in significant loss of land, as well as damage to infrastructure such as roads, buildings, and ground water resources.

### The right-hand side (right-side) is particularly vulnerable without adequate protection, having been subject to piecemeal engineering along its entire length, which has since mostly failed or collapsed. As such, this section of coastline is routinely over-washed by waves during storms, resulting in significant erosion of the land and loss of infrastructure. It also serves as a key evacuation route for the area. In Korotoga (right side), the shoreline is functional but very low lying, and it too, is regularly subject to piecemeal engineering which has failed or collapsed over time. The right-hand side is particularly vulnerable without adequate protection, having been subject to piecemeal engineering along its entire length, which has since mostly failed or collapsed.

### It is particularly important to protect from increasing severity of storm waves and sea level rise, since if this shoreline fails and the land is lost, it will be difficult to defend against future storms. If this the potential to flood the entire blue zone where the main villages of Manuku, Navutuaki and Talafou are located.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

## Figure B Change along the Kolonga proposed road site shown over elevation profile

This narrow ~2km slightly raised beach feature doubles as a road and power line easement and is the main connecting road between Manuka (to the North) and Kolonga (to the South). The Kolonga shoreline is one of the most extensive freshwater swamps landsward. Whilst this nearshore feature is slightly more elevated than the Nukulaeke shoreline to the east, it is still extremely vulnerable to over wash and damage. In both cases these shorelines are extremely vulnerable to over wash and the very old Nukulaeka shoreline has been eroded away in places. The Kolonga shoreline is also subject to erosion due to the construction of a road (labelled as Hoversons poor engineering) for at least 60 years; thereby there are few environmental alternatives to replacing the failing ad hoc concrete and gravel road with a new development that is able to effectively reduce over wash and damage to property and assets such as the single access road.

### 31. Kolonga

The Kolonga shoreline site is also very exposed to over wash events and road failure can be expected in the near future. The Kolonga shoreline has been eroded by overwash events since the mid-20th century, with the most recent event being the cyclone of March 2005. This resulted in the loss of the first 200m after this the shoreline was stabilised using concrete and gravel. However, this is not a permanent solution as the Kolonga shoreline is subject to unusual geological formations, mangrove and seagrass grows in the nearby shallows. As such, it is not deemed adequate to replace the failing road with concrete and gravel. The most effective way of addressing this issue would be to simply raise the road and construct a fenced protective natural rock wall on the seaward edge of the raised road. In this low-lying area of Tongatapu, sea level rise and more intense cyclone events are expected in the future which will also reduce damage to the main connecting road between Manuka and the other western villages and Kolonga. Kolonga is also a key location for tourism and cyclone alerts. Hence the serviceability of this connecting road is in itself an important consideration.

### 32. Tongatapu

Within Tongatapu, there are several locations that have been identified as requiring urgent adaptation measures. In the low-lying areas of Tongatapu, sea level rise and more intense cyclone cycles are causing increased vulnerability to coastal erosion. The most vulnerable location is Manuka, which is located on the western coast of Tongatapu, the target location for physical coastal protection work under this project (considered among the most at-risk locations).

### Previous work addressing adaptation needs

To date, the Government of Tonga has a number of critical policies and strategies in place to address climate change impacts:

- The Joint National Action Plan 2 on Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management (JNAP2) 2018–2030 is the overarching policy document that sets out Tonga’s national approach for addressing climate change. JNAP2 provides a strategic framework for the country to achieve its vision of a Resilient Tonga by 2030. JNAP2 is aligned with the Tonga Climate Change Policy and contributes towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in Tonga, including SDG13 on building resilient communities.

### Resilience-building response capacity (4): Resilience-building actions; (5) Finance and (6) Regional and international cooperation.

These build on Tonga’s first Joint National Action Plan on Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (JNAP), which was developed in 2013, and the Government’s ongoing planning for community levels as well as at the Outer Islands in their planning processes for adaptation planning:

### The Tonga National Infrastructure Investment Plan (TNIIP) (2021–2050) (2021).

This plan is a long-term strategic framework for infrastructure planning and development, and sets out seven National Deltas for: a) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic knowledge-based economy; b) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic social services; c) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic, inclusive, sustainable and empowering human development with gender equality; d) a more inclusive, sustainable and responsive environment; e) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic urbanization and the maintenance of infrastructure and technology; f) is a more inclusive, sustainable and effective land administration, environmental protection and disaster risk management; g) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic economic advancement of our external interests, security and sovereignty.

### The Comprehensive National Development Plan (CNDP), Tonga’s National Planning Framework, including Island Status Development Plans – for five years (2017–2020), (2018–2023), (2019–2024), (2020–2025), and (2021–2026) were developed by the Ministry of Planning, Development and Statistics (MPDS) in collaboration with the Tonga National Planning Commission to streamline strategic planning priorities into a single framework. The Tonga Infrastructure Project Task Force (TIPTF), the Tonga Fisheries Sector Fund Task Force (TFSTF), and the Tonga Agriculture Sector Fund Task Force (TASF) are all part of the TNIIP, as well as the JNAP.

### Migration and Sustainable Development Policy 2020 which provides the Government of Tonga with a policy planning framework to conceptualize and mainstream migration within Tonga’s national development planning in a way that maximizes opportunities for economic growth and social inclusion. The policy is designed to address the challenges posed by migration, including the need to manage the impacts of migration on development; the need to reduce the number of people migrating from Tonga due to climate change; in terms of: Ensure access for all to adequate, safe, and affordable housing and suitable land tenure arrangements; ensure that all migrants have access to education and training opportunities; and promote sustainable urbanization and the capacity for participation, integrated, and sustainable human settlement planning and management. The policy also seeks to address the challenges posed by migration in terms of: Ensure equitable access to economic resources, ownership and control over land and other forms of property, inheritance, and natural resources in order to reduce vulnerability and enhance resilience.

### The Tonga National Infrastructure Investment Plan 2021–2050 (TNIIP) was approved by the Cabinet in August 2021 and sets out a long-term strategic framework for infrastructure planning and development. The TNIIP is based on the investment test practices into the Government of Tonga’s integrated planning and project cycles, which include regular updates to the National Development Plan (NDP), the National Budget, the National Strategic Plan (NSP) 2018–2025, and each an administration’s Government Priority Agenda (GPA). The Tonga National Infrastructure Investment Plan (TNIIP) is a long-term strategic framework for infrastructure planning and development. It sets out seven national Deltas for: a) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic knowledge-based economy; b) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic social services; c) more inclusive, sustainable and empowering human development with gender equality; d) a more inclusive, sustainable and responsive environment; e) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic urbanization and the maintenance of infrastructure and technology; f) is a more inclusive, sustainable and effective land administration, environmental protection and disaster risk management; g) more inclusive, sustainable and dynamic economic advancement of our external interests, security and sovereignty.

### National Women’s Empowerment and Gender Equality Tonga Policy and Strategic Plan Act 2019-2025 sets out to achieve gender equity 2040, and ensures that “All women, men, girls and boys have a whole-accessive equal access to the economic resources, ownership and control over land and other forms of property, inheritance, and natural resources in order to reduce vulnerability and enhance resilience. It also seeks to ensure that women are provided with opportunities and support for women to maximize their participation and benefits from society.

### The Kingdom of Tonga National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP) 2016 sets out to achieve the CBD Convention’s Aichi Targets by 2020. It also seeks to mainstream biodiversity conservation, amongst others with a strong emphasis on ecosystem approaches, adaptation and resilience building.

### The Kingdom of Tonga Rise to Reef Adaptation Framework Act 2020-2030 under the GEF Pacific International Waters Project (PIW) aims to reduce vulnerability in coastal communities through the development of national policy frameworks, development activities and community-led initiatives to ensure that water resources are managed sustainably.

### 34. In response to Tonga’s urgent need for building coastal resilience to climate change and in accordance with JNAP priorities, including the need to build a more resilient coastal zone, the Government has developed a project proposal under the ADB Multi hazard Assessment (MHA) 2021-2025 to assess potential natural hazards in Tonga’s outer islands. The project will also include the development of a Technical Assistance Project (TAP) and a Long-term Adaptation Strategy and Resilience Sector Project (LARSRP). ADs plan to support the Government to manage current flood risk and increase the resilience of coastal communities through the implementation of a multi-hazard assessment, technical assistance and investment project. The project will develop, including a long term adaptation strategy for Nuku’alofa which will enable continued development in the outer islands while ensuring that Tonga’s coastal zone is resilient to climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

B

### Other projects such as the Australian Government's Governance for Resilient Development in the Pacific Program (G4RDP) aim to strengthen national, provincial, district and local governments and communities, as well as regional organizations, to strengthen decision-making processes and governance systems towards improved disaster risk management. The G4RDP also aims to build capacity of national and sub-national governments to technical capacity building and training for government staff, for climate-risk informed decision making under Output 3.

- **Tonga Climate Resilience Sector Project (CRSP), Asian Development Bank, 2015-2019:** This project was funded by the ADB and implemented in partnership with the Government of Tonga. The CRSP aimed to build resilience against climate change through a multi-sector approach. It focused on three key areas: water resources management, disaster risk reduction, and coastal protection.

- **Pacific Climate Change Program for Climate Information (PCCIC):** The PCCIP project contributed 20% of funding for coastal protection measures in the Pacific region. It aimed to improve climate information services and support decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation.

- **Tonga National Adaptation Plan (NAP) Development:** Completed in 2017, supported the development of 11 Community Development Plans (CDPs) between 2015 and 2017, supporting 3,238 direct beneficiaries.

- **Enabling Climate Resilience for Urbanization and Sustainable Building Practices (PACRES), 2016 – 2022:** working with the Ministry of Meteorology, Energy, Information, Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change, Agriculture, Forestry, Fisheries, Water Resources, and Tourism. The project aimed to enhance Tonga’s climate resilience focused on the development of climate change and disaster risk reduction information-and-knowledge-based systems for urban planning and building practices.

- **Tonga's climate change context hosted by NEDDEC:** (https://www.tongaclimatemap.org/ Output 2 supported the climate change adaptation strategy, which was developed under PACRES. The project aimed to enhance Tonga’s understanding of the impact of climate change developed under PACRES by providing the critical technical capacity for tools and for climate information services.

- **The Global Climate Change Alliance Plus Scaling Up Adaptation Program (GGCA+SUAP), 2019-2020:** The overall objective is to enhance Tonga’s ability to adapt to climate change. The specific objective is to better equip communities to understand small scale coastal protection measures that hard and soft engineering, as well as the importance of mangrove ecosystems in providing natural coastal protection.

- **Tongatapu Coastal Protection Measures in Western Tongatapu Project:** European Union, 2016 – 2018: Funding by the European Union (EUR 530,000) and implemented by GIZ. This project has the overall objective of increasing resilience of coastal communities to climate change through the implementation of a combination of hard and soft engineering measures for coastal protection and management.

- **Tongatapu Coastal Protection Measures in Eastern Tongatapu Project:** European Union, 2013 – 2015: This project was part of the GIZ funded Pacific Climate Change Program (PCCIP) and implemented by the Pacific Community (SPC). The project focused on designing, building and monitoring “hard” and soft engineering measures for coastal protection. The first measure consisted of the construction of permeable mangrove groves coupled with beach replenishment and coastal planting. The second measure involved the construction of permeable mangrove groves coupled with beach replenishment and coastal planting.

- **The European Union funded GGCA+ Project in Tonga is “Trailing” coastal protection measures in eastern Tongatapu 2019 – 2023:** This project was part of the GGCA+SUAP and implemented by GIZ. The project aimed to enhance Tonga’s ability to adapt to climate change through a combination along two coastal stretches. One measure consists of the construction of permeable mangrove groves coupled with beach replenishment and coastal planting, while another measure involves the construction of permeable mangroves combined with beach replenishment and coastal planting.

- **The Pacific Regional Program for Climate Change Adaptation (PRCP), USAID and Land and Learn Environment Education 2012-2018:** The PRCP was a US $14 million program aimed at helping build the national and regional risk governance framework in the Pacific region. It also aimed to enhance Tonga’s ability to adapt to climate change through a combination of hard and soft engineering measures for coastal protection.

The Tonga Green Climate Fund (TGF) project is designed to enhance climate resilience and promote sustainable development in Tonga by implementing a series of projects that address the impacts of climate change on natural resources, livelihoods, and infrastructure.

### B.2 (a). Theory of Change narrative diagram and text (max. 1300 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

The theory of change (ToC) diagram is an important tool for visualizing how a project will achieve its intended outcomes over time. It provides a clear picture of the relationships between activities, outputs, and outcomes by using real-life chain links from activities to outcomes, to outputs. By referring to the ToC diagram alongside the narrative description, the TGF team can better understand the logic behind the project's design.

The ToC diagram and narrative may include a wide range of co-benefits as applicable in the context of the project. Note all co-benefits will need to be further elaborated in section D.3 (sustainable development potential) and corresponding co-benefit indicators should be identified under sections E.5 (project/programme specific criteria).

The ToC diagram should also be supported by sections E.3 (risks, regulations, technical, financial, ecological, institutional, etc.) that will need to be addressed as well as risks and assumptions. Note that the assumptions can be elaborated further in section E.4 (assumptions and increased resilience) and E.5 (project/programme specific criteria) for each relevant indicator, as appropriate.

TGF categories co-benefits are as cross which are environmental, social, economic, gender, adaptation (relevant to pure mitigation projects), and migration (relevant to pure adaptation projects). Further guidance is available in the funding proposal (FP) guidance note.

### Project Activities

- Activity 1: Capacity Building
    - Activity 2: Policy Advocacy
        - Activity 3: Community Engagement

### Outputs

- Strengthened capacity and engagement for project implementation
- Enhanced national policy framework and coordination
- Enhanced community engagement and participation

### Impact

- Enhanced resilience to climate change impacts, including increased adaptive capacity in coastal communities and long-term climate risk informed adaptation planning.
- National policies and strategies better aligned with a low-carbon, climate-resilient development pathway.
- Increased resilience of vulnerable coastal communities and transformative adaptation to the direct impacts of climate change in Tonga.

### 36. The Theory of Change for this project, shown schematically above, demonstrates how the implementation of project activities will lead to the desired outputs and impact. This is achieved through a combination of short-term capacity building and long-term climate risk informed adaptation planning. It will do so through addressing key barriers to effective adaptation planning and policy development in Tonga, including limited technical knowledge and skills; lack of awareness and understanding of unique climate risks (e.g., sea level rise, increased storm surges, and extreme weather events); and weak institutional capacity for long-term climate risk informed adaptation planning. The Theory of Change also highlights the need to address power relations by transforming them into more just, sustainable or resilient states.

### 37. Organisations are increasingly aware that risk, and impacts due to change have challenged Tonga and brought the issue of transformative adaptation to the forefront. However, adaptive capacity gaps remain to support a deliberative transformational adaptation agenda in the short term.

### 38. Limited capacities within the public sector may affect agenda setting, and the ability to anticipate, guide, and make decisions on climate change issues.

### 39. Limited vertical and horizontal coordination between different levels of government that limits policy and implementation outcomes.

### 40. Inadequate resource allocation to enhance overall resilience, specifically at the community level:

- Lack of location knowledge and discussion on climate change, as well as an understanding and awareness of unique climate risks (e.g., sea level rise, increased storm surges, extreme weather events) in Tonga.
- A diverse range of values and beliefs that may not be in line with the long-term adaptation needs (including related to land use).

### 41. The overall objective is to support a transformative adaptation agenda setting through a participatory process and build policy consensus, and promote planning coherence.

Towards this end, the project will focus on a bottom-up development planning approach that integrates climate change considerations into sectoral strategic plans for land-use and spatial planning and land use management to promote transformational adaptation.

## Framework for Engagement on Transboundary Adaptation

### Planning
- **Mainstreaming role of climate change in land-use planning**
- **Enhancing coherence and coordination between national, provincial, and local authorities**
- **Guidance on integrating climate change into coastal spatial planning and development**

### Capacity Development
- **Coordination for mainstreaming climate change into land-use planning**
- **Support to provinces and districts for capacity building of local communities in adaptation planning**
- **Support to provinces and districts for capacity building of local communities in adaptation planning**

44. Key barriers in the baseline addressed by the project: In the context of Tonga's existing programmes and commitments to reduce its vulnerability to climate change, there are several barriers that must be addressed in order to bring about transboundary adaptation.

Barrier 1: Lack of climate-informed land-use planning, including dialogue and discussions regarding solutions for internal migration and relocation. Climate change is expected to cause a significant increase in sea level rise, with the potential for surges under different climate scenarios and return periods. In Tonga, some coastal communities located in highly exposed locations are already experiencing increased storm surge events and saltwater intrusion into freshwater supplies.

Barrier 2: Limited capacity of local community actors to design and develop bottom-up community plans for implementing climate adaptation measures. The current approach is top-down, with the government leading the process. However, currently, efforts at incorporating climate risk so that communities can undertake adaptation planning are limited.

Output 1: Address this barrier by strengthening national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of climate risks in Tonga's coastal areas. This will include capacity building on climate change vulnerability assessments, risk monitoring and coastal adaptation planning, providing key capacity building training, and mentoring for coastal adaptation planning. The project will also support the development of a national coastal adaptation plan based on a multi-sectorial base of multi-sectorial-based multisectorialism on adaptation planning strategies for long-term resilient planning and development.

Output 2: Address this barrier by strengthening national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of climate risks in Tonga's coastal areas. This will include capacity building on climate change vulnerability assessments, risk monitoring and coastal adaptation planning, providing key capacity building training, and mentoring for coastal adaptation planning. The project will also support the development of a national coastal adaptation plan based on a multi-sectorial base of multi-sectorial-based multisectorialism on adaptation planning strategies for long-term resilient planning and development.

Output 3: Address this barrier by strengthening national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of climate risks in Tonga's coastal areas. This will include capacity building on climate change vulnerability assessments, risk monitoring and coastal adaptation planning, providing key capacity building training, and mentoring for coastal adaptation planning. The project will also support the development of a national coastal adaptation plan based on a multi-sectorial base of multi-sectorial-based multisectorialism on adaptation planning strategies for long-term resilient planning and development.

Barrier 3: There is a significant gap within government ministries to collect, analyse and use climate risk data that is required mainstream climate risks into national policy design and coastal planning. While government agencies have a clear need for climate risk information in their decision-making processes, there are limitations on the availability of this data at all levels.

Output 3: Address this barrier by strengthening national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of climate risks in Tonga's coastal areas. This will include capacity building on climate change vulnerability assessments, risk monitoring and coastal adaptation planning, providing key capacity building training, and mentoring for coastal adaptation planning. The project will also support the development of a national coastal adaptation plan based on a multi-sectorial base of multi-sectorial-based multisectorialism on adaptation planning strategies for long-term resilient planning and development.

Output 4: Address this barrier by strengthening national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of climate risks in Tonga's coastal areas. This will include capacity building on climate change vulnerability assessments, risk monitoring and coastal adaptation planning, providing key capacity building training, and mentoring for coastal adaptation planning. ## B.3 Project programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

Define the project/programme. Describe the proposed set of components, outputs and activities that will be undertaken by the project/programme to address the climate change adaptation challenges described in Section B.1. Note: Please also describe how the programme/project activities will address the barriers defined in Section B.2.

This section of the funding proposal should be consistent with Sections C.2 (clarifying component), E.5 (project/government programme level engagement plan) and G.3 (baseline).

Referring to the feasibility study, describe why this set of interventions was selected instead of alternative solutions and how the project/programme can be used for the needed action in a cost-effective manner. Also identify risks of not taking these actions or if they are applicable.

### 48. The project objective is to build the long-term resilience of vulnerable coastal communities to the direct impacts of climate change, including sea-level rise and increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, through awareness raising, recognition, and long-term risk informed land use and adaptation planning. The project therefore aims to build a long-term resilience of vulnerable coastal communities in Tonga by strengthening their capacity for climate change adaptation through awareness-raising activities, participatory community work, long-term coastal zone informed adaptation planning, and engagement on long-term adaptation solutions.

This objective will be achieved through the three main related Outputs:

#### Output 1: Awareness raising and recognition

- **Output description:** The project will raise awareness of the vulnerability of Tongan communities to climate change hazards. Available climate change projections suggest that in future, the combined effects of sea-level rise and frequent heavy rainfall events will result in increased coastal erosion and flooding in many parts of Tonga, particularly in the country with which to facilitate adaptive response options (including voluntary retreat if appropriate) because of the vulnerability of its communities to these hazards. The project will raise awareness among all stakeholders on this intention, negotiation and regulate that ensures an iterative process that will continuously take into consideration socio-political and economic factors, as well as climate change projections, in order to ensure a long-term vision for Tonga with a long-term vision is possible with a long, detailed process of raising awareness among all parties including the government, civil society organizations, communities, and other stakeholders. The project will also raise awareness on the importance of voluntary retreats in Tonga, and while preparedness planning for such a context is still limited, this situation is very likely to be disastrous.

#### Output 2: Coastal zone informed adaptation planning

- **Output description:** The project will support Tongan communities to develop long-term coastal zone adaptation plans. This output is expected to make first steps required for the Tongan society to move towards making the necessary changes in land use and development patterns that are consistent with climate change projections, and to take a linear process of co-creation and implementation of practical solutions for better land use and water supply. The approach will be based on participatory community work, long-term coastal zone informed adaptation planning, and engagement around transformative adaptation.

#### Output 3: National multi-stakeholder engagement platform

- **Output description:** The project will establish a national multi-stakeholder engagement platform for dialogue on how to achieve long-term effective climate change adaptation strategies and solutions including voluntary retreat. The project will develop a multi-sectoral stakeholder engagement plan and guidelines for a bottom-up national engagement process, by undertaking the following sub-activities:
  - **Sub-Activity 1.1.1 Design of a multi-sectoral and multi-actor stakeholder engagement plan and guidelines for a bottom-up national engagement process:**

## Sub-Activity 1.1.3 Facilitate a national awareness campaign on climate change and disaster risks and long-term adaptation strategies for multi-stakeholder dialogues co-creating long-term adaptation solutions and strategies (using existing platforms)

### Sub-Activity 1.1.4 Develop a Terms of Reference for a National adaptation planning platform specifying methods, procedures and timelines

#### Sub-Activity 1.1.5 Undertake meetings of the national engagement platform on long term climate change adaptation as per the Terms of Reference

#### Sub-Activity 1.1.6 Develop policy options to incorporate green resilient land use and land use priorities into national frameworks

The design of the multi-stakeholder engagement platform for dialogue on long-term climate change adaptation strategies and solutions will be based on the findings from the stakeholder engagement plan developed specifically for this bottom-up national engagement process. The dialogue will be organized in a series of meetings, workshops and seminars that will be carried out based on the findings of the stakeholder engagement plan developed specifically for the national dialogue stage. The dialogue will be organized into three phases: (i) awareness raising; (ii) policy development; and (iii) implementation.

The awareness campaign will utilize the data and information gathered and developed on the risks and vulnerabilities, future climate scenarios and adaptation options. Output 6 of this proposal will provide a detailed plan for the awareness campaign activities under output 1.1.

## 55. Output 2 Strengthened national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of climate risks

65. Output 3 addresses the limited capacity and available data to collect, analyze and use climate risk information consistent strategic planning and budgeting processes. The Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Communications (MECC) is responsible for managing and monitoring coastal protection. These are the Ministries of Land, Survey and Natural Resources (MLSNR), Meteorological Department (MD) and the National University of Tonga (NUT). The MLNR has a role in land use planning and the Meteorological Department for climate monitoring. These agencies and the Government of Tonga in general do not currently have the capacity to collect, analyze and use climate risk information consistent with strategic planning and budgeting activity therefore aims to build capacity in relation to climate data interpretation in order to allow for better informed coastal management.

### 56. The Capacity Needs Assessment will assess the key institutional capacity needs in the fields of technical capacity for coastal monitoring and analysis, including training and equipment needs, as well as financial capacity needed to implement and implementing the measures that are within technical and financial capacities of the Government. The Capacity Needs Assessment will be conducted by a team from the Ministry of Internal Affairs (MIA) with support from MECC. This assessment will be based on a review of existing climate risk information systems and tools, including government use on coastal and climate risk monitoring and analysis to inform coastal adaptation planning (e.g., including vulnerability assessments, hazard mapping, etc.). The assessment will also include an assessment of the capacity needs for technical training in climate change risk monitoring and analysis. This will be conducted through a) a review of existing training materials and b) a review of the current training needs of government staff involved in coastal management activities. The assessment will also include an assessment of the capacity needs for technical training in climate change risk mapping and analysis, including vulnerability assessments, hazard mapping, etc., as well as the need to incorporate these findings into long-term settlement patterns and climate risk strategy for long-term adaptation planning.

Under the Capacity Building Strategy Plan, technical officers and local government officials will be trained on coastal zone management, monitoring and analysis of coastal hazards. Training will include training in coastal zone management and understanding and training materials developed and distributed to support government staff across ministries to be able to interpret and use climate risk information consistent with strategic planning and budgeting processes. The training will be conducted by a team from the Ministry of Internal Affairs (MIA) with support from MECC, and will include a package of training and capacity building relevant to the specific ministries that equipment and data can be fully utilized for coastal monitoring and analysis. Training materials developed as part of this project will be available on the MIA website and will cover topics such as climate change risk mapping and analysis, including vulnerability assessments, hazard mapping and topography. Training materials and training materials will be available to support government staff engaged in coastal management activities.

The Government to effectively collect, manage and analyze coastal monitoring data (Sub-Activity 2.1.3 Collecting, interpreting and using data on climate risks for coastal adaptation planning to promote disaster resilience in the Pacific Islands) through a) Sub-Activity 2.1.2 Strengthening capacity of the Ministry of Internal Affairs for use under Output 1.1; and b) Sub-Activity 2.1.3 Improve multi-stakeholder coordination and collaboration on coastal adaptation and planning.

### Activity 2.1: Strengthened mechanisms for collecting and analyzing data and information for better informed climate risk management

* **Sub-Activity 2.1.1 Increase capacity of the Government to effectively collect, manage and analyze coastal monitoring data**
* **Sub-Activity 2.1.2 Collecting, interpreting and using data on climate risks for coastal adaptation planning to promote disaster resilience in the Pacific Islands**
* **Sub-Activity 2.1.3 Improve multi-stakeholder coordination and collaboration on coastal adaptation and planning integrating climate change**

Tonga has no access to the fundamental baselines required to understand the impacts of sea level rise and marine hazards, namely high quality, accurate, nearshore bathymetry (sea floor depth) and topography (land elevation). It is crucial that the government invest in collecting this data. The lack of such data makes it difficult for Tonga to assess its vulnerability to climate change and coastal hazards. This is because there are no reliable maps or charts available on which to base decisions about where to build new infrastructure, how to protect existing infrastructure from sea level rise and wave impacts, etc. It is not possible to design detailed adaptation plans for marine hazards without this data. The government has been unable to collect the necessary data due to a lack of funding. Because of the need for high quality data for considering sea level rise or wave impacts, The Project intends to support Tonga in collecting and analyzing coastal monitoring data through a) Sub-Activity 2.1.2 Collecting, interpreting and using data on climate risks for coastal adaptation planning to promote disaster resilience in the Pacific Islands; b) Sub-Activity 2.1.3 Improve multi-stakeholder coordination and collaboration on coastal adaptation and planning integrating climate change.

The Project intends to support Tonga in collecting and analyzing coastal monitoring data through a) Sub-Activity 2.1.2 Collecting, interpreting and using data on climate risks for coastal adaptation planning to promote disaster resilience in the Pacific Islands; b) Sub-Activity 2.1.3 Improve multi-stakeholder coordination and collaboration on coastal adaptation and planning integrating climate change.

73. At the southwestern end of the Nukuleka Peninsula, 2.3 km of rock revetment will be installed along the coast between the villages of Taimini and Nukuleka to protect the low-lying and highly vulnerable shoreline. On the north-eastern side, coastal protection measures for the Manuka to Kolonga shoreline will consist of over topping protection but not a foreshore revetment. In comparison with the northern section of the Nukuleka Peninsula, this section has been subject to extensive foreshore revetment works. This section of coast remains as an important habitat for marine life and is more resilient than other sections of the Nukuleka Peninsula shoreline where the shore is stressed and in places erode. It has a greater measure of ecosystem integrity and resilience than the Nukuleka Peninsula shoreline to the north, which has been extensively designed (and failing) structures constructed over decades and has no residual soft shoreline left due to its erosive nature.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 30

## Figure 11 (Shoreline between Nukuleka and Tafalala)

### Caption: Coastal shore Nukuleka - Tafalala

74. The Kolonga section will consist of raising the roadway easement (approximately 1m but to be determined via modelling) and rescaling the surface to remitiate the eroded roadway. A two-dimensional wave modeling to determine wave height and run-up dynamics is required to determine the extent of erosion. The raised road would also be armored with local materials as a mostly buried line of defense given it is positioned on a steep slope. This will ensure that the road remains stable in the event of large waves and storm surges. The road is located within an area of agricultural importance, and the objective is to protect this land from erosion while also ensuring that the existing ecological integrity of this area of coast is undisturbed and can continue to provide critical services of ecosystem function such as coastal protection, carbon sequestration, and provision of fresh groundwater supply. The raised roadway would also prevent routine cutting of this road during wave events as sand, rocks, and other debris are frequently thrown towards the road by waves. This will also protect the emergency evacuation route (e.g., tsunami warning) and also protect the mains electricity lines which run parallel to the road.

## Figure 14: Comparison of the current situation (Panel 1) and shoreline protection construction (Panel 2) of 2m of coastal protection measures developed for Manuku to Kotogana shoreline

### 1
![Image description](image_description_1.png)

### 2
![Image description](image_description_2.png)

For the shoreline between Manuku and Kotogana, the road surface would be raised approximately 1.5m to provide overtopping protection equivalent to the ADB investment completed in 2018. The seaward face would be excavated to a hard planed platform and then backfilled with sand to create a stable base for the new road surface. This will allow for the construction of a hard seaward face and soft landward face, which is more cost-effective than constructing a hard seaward and landward face. The seaward face would be constructed using concrete block designs. Finally, the impervious freshwater swamp, tree cropping, and tar growing areas, would be protected from marine erosion by planting mangroves on the seaward side of the road.

76. In order to obtain the necessary permits for construction, the following steps are required: a) The area of development is Government land and confirmation with MSLF for a Development Consent permit still is required per the Act. The application will also require the submission of an Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA). b) The EIA will be prepared by the Project Technical Staff in accordance with sections 32 and 36 of the National Spatial Planning and Management Act 2012. The Development Consent for Coastal Protection Measures will be issued by MSLF after the EIA has been approved. c) The project will require a formal specification and design drawings. d) Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA). e) Land ownership information/confirmation, if required. f) A detailed plan of the works to be carried out on the land. g) A list of all capital works to be undertaken. h) A statement that all project capital works are located over the topsoil of existing government infrastructure easements. Nonetheless a formal specification and design drawings will be prepared by the Project Technical Staff in accordance with sections 32 and 36 of the National Spatial Planning and Management Act 2012.

77. An Operation and Maintenance Plan specific to the coastal protection measures will be developed as part of the design of the measures, which will be included within the Coastal Protection Measures Design Report. This report will form a key component of the Project Strategy and Plan which will be developed for the post-construction G&M by the Design and Supervision Engineers following the completion of the construction works. The Operation and Maintenance Plan will be prepared in conjunction with the Design and Supervision Engineering Firm. It will be part of the handover of the assets to the responsible Government agency, which will include a manual and knowledge tool for the Government together with what will be produced under Activity 3.1 to sustain the technical knowledge and lessons learnt for coastal protection measures.

### B

78. **Activity 3.2: Synthesis of lessons learned and best practices in climate resilient coastal protection measures for scale-up at the national and regional level**

In such a diverse region, where effective coastal protection measures are limited in number and diversity. The limitation is even more severe when one considers design standards that incorporate future climate risks. The coastal protection measures implemented under Activity 3.2 will be evaluated to determine their effectiveness in terms of cost-effectiveness, resilience, and sustainability. More importantly, this project is considered transformational in its approach of combining short-term and long-term measures on the ground with a regional focus on knowledge sharing and capacity building at the national level for scale-up across the region.

Overall lessons of the technical effectiveness of the coastal protection measures implemented in Activity 3.2, i.e., the cost-effectiveness, resilience, and sustainability will be synthesized into best practices and lessons learned to be shared within the region. These lessons will be disseminated through regional/national/international conference proceedings and other media, as well as providing guidelines and standardizer manuals for coastal protection measures in the region. The lessons learned from this project will also be used by the GCF to inform its future investments in coastal protection.

The experience gained under this activity is expected to contribute to building of adaptation capacity throughout Tonga at varied levels, government academic institutions and NGOs community representatives, through their active participation in the Conference event as a knowledge sharing platform. The lessons learned from this project will also be used by the GCF to inform its future investments in coastal protection.

The Conference will also contribute to building of adaptation capacity throughout Tonga at varied levels, government academic institutions and NGOs community representatives, through their active participation in the Conference event as a knowledge sharing platform. The lessons learned from this project will also be used by the GCF to inform its future investments in coastal protection.

88. Roles and Responsibilities of the Project Organisation Structure

The Project Board/Steering Committee is the senior, dedicated oversight body for a project. The Project Board is responsible for making, by consensus, management decisions when guidance is required by the Project Manager in order to ensure UNDP's ultimate accountability.

Project Board decisions should be made in accordance with standards that shall ensure management for development results best value, fairness, harmony, integrity and transparent and effective international competition.

The three prominent (mandatory) roles of the project are:

* High-level decision making is performed by the Implementing Partner: UNDP. (as explained in the "Project Overview" section of the POPP). This is the primary function of the project board and includes annual project reviews, mid-term reviews, final reviews, project adjustments, corrective actions or remedial measures to address them effectively. The Project Board reviews evidence of project performance against the approved project document and makes decisions on any necessary changes and the continued delivery report. The Project Board is the main body responsible for taking corrective action as needed in accordance with the POPP.
* Approval of strategic project execution decisions of the Implementing Partner: UNDP, with a view to assess and manage risks associated with project implementation. This includes approval of annual work plans and mid-term reviews, and long term sustainability of project execution decisions of the Implementing Partner (as explained in the "Monitor Output" section of the POPP).
* The Project Board is responsible for approval of multi-year and annual workplans and budgets. The multi-year workplan and budget are prepared by UNDP and approved by the Project Board at least once a year.
* Any changes and reallocation of resources will be first reviewed and approved by the project board and communicated to the implementing partner.

89. The Project Board is responsible for providing overall guidance and direction to the project, ensuring it remains within any specified budgetary limits and that all project activities are in line with the POPP. This includes approving the review-project performance based on monitoring, evaluation and reporting, including progress reports, risk register and the combined delivery report. The Project Board also makes decisions regarding the project's financial accountability. Project Board decisions are made in accordance with standards that shall ensure management for development results best value fairness, harmony, integrity and transparent and effective international competition. Decisions must be reached within the Board; the UNDP representative on the board will moderate to find consensus and if this cannot be found, the UNDP Director General will make a decision.

90. The Project Board undertakes Risk Management, by providing guidance or evolving or materialized project risks and agrees on risk mitigation measures. This includes approving the review-project performance based on monitoring, evaluation and reporting, including progress reports, risk register and associated management plans based on the information prepared by the Implementing Partner. This includes approving the review-project performance based on monitoring, evaluation and reporting, including progress reports, risk register and associated management plans based on the information prepared by the Implementing Partner.

91. Project Assurance: Project assurance is the responsibility of each project board member; however, UNDP has a distinct role in ensuring that the project carries out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. UNDP performs the quality assurance function for the project to ensure that all project activities are carried out as per the approved POPP and that all project milestones are managed and completed. The Project Board cannot delegate any of its quality assurance responsibilities to the Project Management Function or any other party. This includes approving the review-project performance based on monitoring, evaluation and reporting, including progress reports, risk register and associated management plans at both project and headquarters levels. Project assurance is totally independent of the Project Management function. A designated representative from UNDP will be responsible for this role as a non-voting member. It should be noted that while in certain cases UNDP's project assurance role across the project may be shared with other parties, it remains the responsibility of the Project Board to ensure that the quality assurance function must, as part of their duties, attend specially called board meetings and provide board members with all required information.

92. Frequency and Companion of the Project Board: The project board will meet at least once annually towards the end of the implementation year (November) to review progress and approve the next year's workplan. The composition of the Project Board may be changed as necessary, but it must always include at least one representative from each of the stakeholders who will ultimately benefit from this project. Their primary function within the board is to ensure the realization of project results for all stakeholders in a manner that ensures best value and fairness. This can be done by either directly benefiting government entities benefiting from the project can full fill this role. There can be multiple beneficiary representatives as in a Regional Project Board (RPPB) or at headquarters level. The Project Board may also include other parties such as: (a) Government Partners; (b) Development Partners; (c) Individuals or groups representing the interests of the parties concerned that provide funding, technical assistance and/or expertise to the project; (d) Other relevant stakeholders including representatives from Meteorology, Energy, Information, Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change and Communications (MEDEC).
