The Project Manager (PM) will lead project coordination as the senior most representative of the Project Management Unit (PMU), and is responsible for the overall day-to-day management of the project on behalf of UNDP. Including the preparation of work plans, monitoring progress, and reporting to the Board. The Project Manager will attend all board meetings and support board process as a non-voting representative. The Project Manager will also be the primary point of contact with the Government of Tonga (GOT) and other stakeholders.

## Grant financing is requested from the GCF to cover transformation in the current practices of Tonga's national land use planning towards long-term adaptation focused and climate risk sensitive planning; and to develop an effective climate risk management framework for Tonga, which will help to reduce vulnerability and exposure to climate risks, and protect lives and assets of the vulnerable communities in the most exposed section of the north-eastern Tongan coast.

Although Tonga is not classified as a least developed country (LDC), its economic and environmental vulnerability has increased significantly over recent years due to climate change impacts. The island nation's population is highly concentrated on the low-lying coastal plains, which could increase resilience to disasters at a faster pace." Tonga's population today lives in a dynamic ecosystem where communities are vulnerable to natural hazards such as cyclones, floods and tsunamis. Communities are likely to need support to adapt and manage disaster risks facing their livelihoods, homes, and infrastructure.

The Tongan government has identified the north-eastern coast of Tongatapu Island as one of its most exposed areas to climate change impacts. The area is highly vulnerable due to its low-lying coastal plains, which are prone to flooding from sea level rise, storm surges, and tsunamis. The area also faces increased risk of cyclones and other extreme weather events.

Tonga is a Small Developing Island State (SDIS) with a total population of 109,179, **which is considered to be highly vulnerable to climate change impacts**. The country's vulnerability stems from its small size, low-lying coastal plains, and high exposure to natural hazards such as cyclones, floods, and tsunamis.

Tonga has also experienced a series of climate disasters in recent years; tropical cyclone Ian (2014), Hovisit (2018), Hiraiti (2020); as well as the 2015-2016 El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) event, which caused severe flooding and coastal erosion. The country has also experienced waves through the Tonga Archipelago. Combined, climate-induced and non-climate related disasters affected up to 84% of Tonga's population.

Notably, during recent years Tonga's economy has been affected by both climate and non-climate related disaster impacts; with a significant number of households losing their homes due to flooding and coastal erosion. The country's tourism sector, which is the main driver of its economy previously suffered from climate-related impacts in the form of severe devastation from Tropical Cyclone (TC) Gita on 12 February 2015.

The Tonga government has identified the north-eastern coast of Tongatapu Island as one of its most exposed areas to climate change impacts. The area is highly vulnerable due to its low-lying coastal plains, which are prone to flooding from sea level rise, storm surges, and tsunamis. The area also faces increased risk of cyclones and other extreme weather events.

Tonga has also experienced a series of climate disasters in recent years; tropical cyclone Ian (2014), Hovisit (2018), Hiraiti (2020); as well as the 2015-2016 El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) event, which caused severe flooding and coastal erosion. The country has also experienced waves through the Tonga Archipelago. Combined, climate-induced and non-climate related disasters affected up to 84% of Tonga's population.

Given the combined factors of high upliftment requirements for coastal adaptation, and the high levels of Tonga's vulnerability to climate change impacts, it is imperative that a long-term coastal resilience strategy be developed in order to address the short- term coastal resistance and protection management. Efforts to date have been undertaken on a case by case and programme basis.

107. The following sub-activities of the project under Outputs 1 and 2, support capacity and technology development/transfer, specifically:

Output 1 Strengthen national and local capacities for effective monitoring and assessment of climate risks and adaptation planning, including coastal risk management and coastal adaptation planning; and (2) improve the knowledge base of multi-sectoral stakeholders on climate change impacts and vulnerability to climate change in order to support climate adaptation for Tonga based on climate risks and projections. This output enhances the mechanism for policy dialogue and decision-making at national level by providing a platform for sharing information, building capacity, and promoting key capacity-building, training and mentoring for coastal adaptation planning and monitoring and development of coastal adaptation plans. The project will also provide technical assistance to the Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC) in developing a sectoral framework on climate change adaptation for Tonga, including the development of a national coastal adaptation plan. This includes improving LGDRs and preparing them for submission to SPC. The project will also develop a training manual on scenario-based coastal risk maps, for decisions on long-term settlement patterns and climate risk for future generations. The project will also provide technical assistance in developing a national coastal adaptation and planning integrating climate change. MRDN and MDECC do not currently have access to the latest information on climate risks and vulnerability.

This activity therefore aims to build capacity in relation to climate risk data/interpretation in order to allow for better decision-making at all levels of government, including local authorities. # CITED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The project is to be the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 1.1 The overall objective of the project is to support the transformational adaptation agenda through setting up a participatory planning and management framework for climate change adaptation at national level in Tonga. This will be achieved on the basis of an integrated approach that includes: (i) strengthening the institutional capacity of the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change, Agriculture and Forestry; (ii) supporting the linking development planning with spatial planning and land use management to promote transformative adaptation through the incorporation of climate change risk information into national strategic plans; and (iii) promoting the integration of climate change adaptation in sectoral planning. The project will be guided by Tonga's National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan, which sets out the intended outcomes for adoption by elaborating on how the project programme contributes to increased climate-resilient development in Tonga.

### 1.2 Indirectly the project benefits the broader population given the community development planning and plans supported through the project. The project will also support the establishment of a national multi-stakeholder engagement platform for dialogue on long-term climate change adaptation strategies, which will be used to inform the development of sectoral plans and other relevant policy documents.

### 1.3 The project is expected to support multiple district-level spatial plans by providing data information and interpretation for incorporation into Tonga's National Strategic Plan (NSP) and the island strategic planning process. This includes: (i) the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy for Tongatapu, the Island Strategic Development Plans – for Ha'apai (2017-2032), Nua (2015-2018), Eua (2016-2019); (ii) the National Coastal Zone Management Plan (2014-2023); (iii) the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy for Vava'u (2017-2020); and (iv) the Island Strategic Development Plans – for Niuatoputapu (2015-2018), Nua (2015-2018), Eua (2016-2019) and Vava'u (2017-2020). The island spatial planning will be supported by the project through the provision of climate change risk information, including vulnerability assessments, hazard maps, coastal risk maps and knowledge material for use.

### 1.4 Directly the project benefits 35% of Tonga's population (i.e., approximately 68,000 people). Of these, 27% are women members) who are therefore expected to directly benefit from being trained in climate-risk adaptation and for whom the project will provide access to training on coastal risk monitoring. The project also provides direct benefits from the project through training in incorporating climate change risks in planning and budgeting, supporting development of sectoral plans, and providing technical assistance to Tonga's Ministry of Environment, Climate Change, Agriculture and Forestry (MECAF) for developing a national coastal zone management plan. The project will provide 10 days of training on coastal risk monitoring and data; 3,258 direct beneficiaries will receive protection through coastal protection measures such as sea walls, groynes, breakwaters, and other erosion control structures; and 4,769 people will be trained in wave and overwaving events (Output:3).

### 1.5 The project contributes to the strengthening and use of information on coastal processes, oceans and climate in decision making by strengthening institutional capacity, human resources, awareness and knowledge for resilient coastal management.

### 1.6 It is designed to reduce the risk of locking finite Government resources into poorly planned infrastructure projects that fail to meet future needs due to rapid changes in sea levels and other climate change impacts. The project will also improve modelling capacity; this project asset helps GCF in avoiding the lock-in of misinvestment. However, the project ensures that Tonga's coastal zone management plan is developed with a focus on climate resilience. Analysis shows that the project will be economically efficient and robust to cost increases and benefits will be shared by all stakeholders.

### 1.7 Finally, the proposed interventions will contribute to a body of knowledge about adaptive resilience. The best practices that will be documented in this project will be disseminated through workshops, seminars, publications and other means for exposure to ongoing climate risks. The mutual reinforcement of both outputs will ensure Tonga has extensive experiences in hard and soft-engineering practices aligned with institutional and community-based knowledge.

# D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page)

## The current paradigm

### Current challenges and opportunities

- **Lack of data and capacity to interpret climate risks to coastal property, infrastructure, and livelihoods**
- **No mechanism for addressing coastal climate risks at community level**
- **Limited understanding of coastal protection infrastructure**

### Future paradigm (desired state)

#### Desired adaptation plans and policy with broad public and political acceptance

- **Strong cohort of officials and community members with capacity to interpret data and communicate effectively**
- **Community development plans incorporate coastal risks and define future resilience pathways**

## The shift process

### On a technical level, the desired focus is on knowledge transfer and building capacity to manage and interpret climate data. This includes training in the use of tools such as the MEEDAC, MLDAC, and MALDAC to monitor coastal wave and current dynamics on a continual basis contributing to the body of knowledge about how these systems are changing over time.

### On an institutional level, the desired focus is on building capacity for climate change adaptation planning. This includes training in the use of tools such as the MEEDAC, MLDAC, and MALDAC to monitor coastal wave and current dynamics on a continual basis contributing to the body of knowledge about how these systems are changing over time.

### On an institutional level, the desired focus is on building capacity for climate change adaptation planning. This includes training in the use of tools such as the MEEDAC, MLDAC, and MALDAC to monitor coastal wave and current dynamics on a continual basis contributing to the body of knowledge about how these systems are changing over time.

### On an institutional level, the desired focus is on building capacity for climate change adaptation planning. This includes training in the use of tools such as the MEEDAC, MLDAC, and MALDAC to monitor coastal wave and current dynamics on a continual basis contributing to the body of knowledge about how these systems are changing over time.

The project will also inform learning across national/sub-national/national community levels within the country and region. The three outputs combined will identify best practices for coastal protection that can facilitate replication of project results beyond the scope of the project itself.

In order to ensure that Tonga is well prepared to adapt to climate change, under different future climate scenarios, the Government of Tonga will improve their capacity to plan and implement interventions on other vulnerable coastlines. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of a national coastal adaptation strategy and guidelines for the implementation of such strategies.

Tonga's experience in comprehensively reintroducing infrastructure, technical, and community barriers through GCF support will be shared with other Pacific Island countries that are interested in adopting similar approaches to their own coastal protection methods.


## 115. AE as one of the outputs of the conference will be organized for the specific purpose of sharing lessons with the international communities, but particularly other SIDS. Prior to the conference, a technical assessment will be carried out to assess the needs and gaps in terms of capacity building and knowledge sharing within the region.

These, and lessons from the national dialogue engagement process, will provide models that offer alternatives to other SIDS.


### The following are some of the expected outcomes:

- Knowledge about future climate risks on coastal areas under different climate scenarios and the need for adaptation strategies.
- Awareness among communities and local authorities in Tonga regarding the importance of internal migration on food security of the country (as people would need to move into areas where there is enough food).
  - Technical knowledge of the construction and monitoring of coastal protection works
  - Awareness about the role of women, youth, and other vulnerable groups in adaptation processes.
- Learning opportunity about community-based vulnerability assessment, adaptation priority setting and on-the-ground implementation.

### 121. The proposed GCF project will contribute towards the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in Tonga:

#### 5.3 Sustainable development

Of particular relevance is the contribution the project makes to SDG Goal 13 on taking urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts, by providing technical assistance for the development of a national coastal adaptation strategy and guidelines for the implementation of such strategies.

The project will also contribute towards SDGs 2 (Zero Hunger), 6 (Clean Water and Sanitation) and 7 (Affordable and Clean Energy). The project will provide technical assistance to Tonga in its efforts to meet its obligations under the Paris Agreement, with respect to Goal 11 making cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable.

The project will also contribute towards SDG 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth) by providing technical assistance and substantially decrease the direct economic losses related to global gross domestic product caused by disasters, including water-related hazards. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of a national coastal adaptation strategy and guidelines for the implementation of such strategies.

#### 120. Economic co-benefits

In addition to the primary benefit of coastal protection, construction under Output 3 of the project will contribute towards SDG Goal 9 (Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure) by providing technical assistance for the construction of investments in north-eastern Tonga. It is expected that approximately 50 short-term jobs will be created within the first year of implementation.

The project will also provide technical assistance to Tonga in its efforts to meet its obligations under the Paris Agreement with respect to Goal 11 making cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable by undertaking skilled labor work including carpentry, heavy machinery operations, survey assistance, etc. This will provide formal employment and earn income opportunities for both aided and low self-sustainable people and have a substantial

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 49

The project has significant environmental co-benefits arising from improved coastal protection, increased biodiversity and reduced erosion in Tonga. The project will also reduce the vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts such as water intrusion into the fragile groundwater resources in Tongatapu, where groundwater is still used for drinking purposes, or aggregate loss due to sea level rise. This will be achieved by constructing a series of coastal protection measures and seawalls that have been previously built but collapsed due to the constant wave energy along the coast of Tonga. However, due to the lack of data on the extent of erosion in some areas, it is not possible to determine the extent of affected sections, and upon which many people rely for their livelihoods. Coastal protection measures that are appropriately designed will reduce the vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts such as water intrusion into the groundwater resources.

The enhanced capacity to monitor coastal systems supported under Output 2 will have wider benefits in terms of improved disaster risk management and climate change adaptation, including the ability to better understand how climate change is impacting coral reefs and potentially apply these skills across other fields. In this way the project support the use of data for evidence-based decision making.

The project will generate a range of social co-benefits that will positively affect the overall well-being of the Tongan people. These include improved access to education, health services, water supply and sanitation facilities, as well as reduced disruptions of economies, agriculture, public and medical services, and cultural activities are directly linked to the levels of coastal erosion and flooding in Tonga. The project will also have a positive impact on the environment by reducing the increased emissions of carbon dioxide from fossil fuel combustion. This is highly limited public health issues in the country. Output 1 will provide the necessary infrastructure for the restoration of these social benefits through the construction of seawalls, while Output 2 will provide the necessary capacity to monitor and manage coastal systems.

The projects constructed under Output 1 will have immediate benefits to the local population. Conversely the fundamental policies emerging from Output 2 will have a long-term impact on the development of Tonga’s economy and its overall population over the medium term. Similarly as the capacity development work and community adaptation planning under Output 1, the project will also provide the necessary infrastructure for the restoration of these social benefits.

Coastal protection in north-eastern Tongatapu will also protect the road used by local communities as the only evacuation route from coastal flooding events. This is particularly important given that this area has experienced severe flooding and undermining the infrastructure, causing a significant risk to the total population. The protection measures will also offer similar benefits for other areas of Tonga where there are no roads or bridges, such as the villages of Nukutapu and Tafuna at sea level rise and erosion go well beyond what is captured in the range of social benefits such as education, health, or even water supply. The project will also provide a significant contribution to the overall development of Tonga by reducing the science of parts of Tonga islands become uninhabitable. Through this project, it ensures integrity irrespective to the gender equality and youth empowerment.

Gender issues will be mainstreamed at all levels during the project implementation in order to ensure promotion of gender equality through women and youth empowerment. The project has taken into consideration the different roles of men and women in Tonga’s society and economy. Women are more vulnerable than men to climate change impacts, particularly as one of the key sources of vulnerability for these groups in this regard; particular focus will be brought to ensuring strong participation by women in all aspects of the project implementation.

The project makes considerable efforts to promote women’s participation and engagement in climate resilience, including gender equality and youth empowerment. The project will also ensure that women are involved in the planning and implementation of local development and spatial planning.

The project has given particular attention paid to increased opportunities by enhancing skills of women and youth in areas of land management activities, climate risk data monitoring and community adaptation planning. Women’s participation is critical for the success of this project as it will be a key component of the overall strategy for community-level planning under Output 1, which will underpin the notion that women are an important stakeholder and agent of change.

Throughout the course of the project, several impact assessments are planned: These assessments offer important information on how the project has contributed to the achievement of its objectives. They also provide a general overview, but also the ways in which women and other interest groups contributed to the achievement of the project impacts.

# D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed project addresses the needs of Tonga’s most vulnerable and beneficiary groups and elaborates how the project programmes address the issue (e.g., the level of exposure to climate risks for beneficiary country and groups, overall income level etc.). Describe below:

* **Vulnerability of the country and/or specific vulnerable subgroups, including gender aspects (if adaptation only)**
* **Economic vulnerability of the country and/or specific vulnerable subgroups**
* **Absence of alternative sources of financing (e.g. fiscal or balance of payments gap) that prevents government from addressing these needs**

## Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

### 130 Settlements in Tonga, are concentrated in coastal areas with significant percentages of the population living and earning their livelihood along the coast. The majority of the settlements are located on low-lying islands, which are vulnerable to sea-level rise, storm surges, and tsunamis. These natural hazards have been exacerbated by climate change, including increased frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones, sea level rise, and coastal erosion caused by flooding during storms. Moreover, freshwater resources are negatively impacted by sea-level rise and increased salinity in the aquifers. The vulnerability is further compounded by Tonga’s low-lying islands, characterized by their small land area with low elevation.

### 131 Large-scale cyclones such as Cyclone Gita, where 4% of the population resides, cause significant loss from flooding, as well as permanent losses from sea level rise (SLR) in the advance of future adaptation measures. The permanent losses associated with SLR are estimated to be 20% of the total land area and 15% of the total population. Tonga has experienced a series of climate disasters over recent years: tropical cyclones in 2014, Gita (2018), Harold (2020); as well as floods and landslides in 2019. The most significant disaster was Cyclone Gita in February 2018, which caused tsunami waves throughout the Tonga archipelago. Combined, climate-induced and non-climate related disasters affected up to 75% of the population in 2018. In addition, sea level rise is projected to increase by 34 cm (±9%) between 2010-2100, characterized by their small land area with low elevation.

### 132 The most vulnerable groups are those living on low-lying islands and in coastal areas. These include the elderly population of over 65 years old, women, children under five years old, people with disabilities, and people who live below the poverty line. In total, these groups make up approximately 40% of Tonga’s population. The most vulnerable are those living on low-lying islands such as Vava’u, Ha’apai, and Tongatapu. These islands have a high proportion of elderly residents (35%), women (61%), children under five years old (28%), people with disabilities (40%), and people who live below the poverty line (79%). The most vulnerable groups are those living on low-lying islands and in coastal areas.

### 133 The limited capacity of Tonga’s government to be led by individuals from small households and small villages. The vast majority of households do not have the financial capital to implement household-level interventions for climate change adaptation. The limited disclosure of information about the vulnerability of Tonga’s most vulnerable groups is a significant barrier in moving forward into longer-term measures for climate resilience. Households are not able to see the long-term consequences, nor are they able to make informed decisions on how best to adapt and prepare themselves against future disasters.

### 135 Although the incidence of absolute poverty is low in Tonga, many experience economic hardships, where they do not have access to basic services and infrastructure such as clean water, sanitation, electricity, and health care. This is due to the high economic cost of the smallness of the country. The national revenue base is heavily influenced by remittances from Tongans living abroad (around 40% of GDP). Remittances are the largest contributor to Tonga’s economy, which has made it one of the world’s smallest economies, as measured by GDP*. Remittances have been the largest contributor to Tonga’s economic growth since independence in 1975. Remittances accounted for around USD 208 million or 37% of GDP in 2020, which increased to 46.2% of GDP in 2021 and decreased again to 37.1% in 2022. The Tonga Ha'apai volcanic eruption and tsunami reduced GDP growth to -2.9%. Although forecasts suggest that real GDP will grow with Tonga's GDP growth expected at 2.5% in 2021, and 3.24% in accordance to the Asia Pacific Economic Outlook (APEC) for 2021-2022, the country is still vulnerable to climate change impacts.

### 134 Tonga's vulnerability to climate change

Tonga is disproportionately burdened with the significant impacts from climate change risks. The root cause of this adverse condition is its high exposure and vulnerability to climate change impacts. This is due to its small size, low-lying islands, limited natural resources, and socio-political factors contribute to its vulnerabilities, and leads to increased risks of climate change impacts in Tonga.

### 135 Economic Impacts of Climate Change

The projected economic impacts of climate change are significant. The SAI can project the short-term cost-prediction effects with GCF funding; long-term resilient capabilities will be expanded so that the SAI can play a more proactive role in reducing vulnerability and enhancing resilience to climate change risks. In addition, for recovery potential, the additional investment required to build resilience to climate change in coastal regions of Tonga is estimated at $10 million. This estimate does not include the cost of building resilience in other sectors, which would significantly reduce the financial viability of the investments beyond project duration.

### 136 The SAI and Climate Change

The SAI has identified key institutional capacity needs in the fields of: Technical capacity to closely monitor the coastal dynamics of vulnerable coastlines; design locally appropriate adaptation measures; and build resilience. Capacity to integrate climate change risks into disaster risk management plans. Capacity to interpret climate change risks and engage in community-led decision making, and Lack of coordination between government entities and other relevant stakeholders.

### 137 Efforts to address climate change vulnerabilities and enhance resilience in Tonga are outlined in the following key national strategies:

* National Climate Change Policy 2016-2030
* National Adaptation Plan (NAP) 2015-2029
* National Disaster Risk Management and Disaster Risk Management 2010-2015 (UNAP)
* Tonga Strategic Communication Report on Climate Change

### 138 Tonga Strategic Development Framework 2015-2020: The Tonga Strategic Development Framework (TDF) in 2015-2020 is a key national strategy that outlines Tonga's development priorities. It focuses on the following areas: Economic diversification, land and environmental management and resilience to climate change, and it is strongly supported by three of five pillars of the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The TDF has been developed in consultation with all relevant stakeholders including government entities, civil society organizations, and other relevant stakeholders. It also includes a focus on vulnerable populations through Community Development Plans (CDPs). To date, CDPs from all 127 communities have been translated into English and are available for download at the Ministry of Finance website. The TDF has established community representatives who chair these committees. In Pillar 4: GCF underscores the importance of building island infrastructure to enhance resilience to climate change impacts. This includes improving water supply systems, enhancing disaster risk management plans, and strengthening community resilience to extreme weather events. Finally, Pillar 5 emphasizes improved land use planning, administration and implementation.

### 139 National Climate Change Policy in 2016: The GCF compiled a comprehensive update to the initial National Initial Climate Change Policy to produce the "National Climate Change Policy: A Roadmap for Tonga" (2016). This is one of five pillars.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 52

The GCF aims to achieve the vision of a resilient Tonga by 2035 through specific environmental, social and economic targets ultimately aimed at increasing Tonga's resilience to the impacts of climate change. The GCF consists the policy framework for the implementation of the NDCs. The GCF is guided by the following principles: (i) equity; (ii) transparency; (iii) accountability; and (iv) efficiency.

The NCDF specifically sets out reducing coastal vulnerability as primary goals. These include: (i) resilient, regenerative, and sustainable livelihoods; (ii) climate-resilient infrastructure; (iii) disaster risk reduction and management; (iv) climate change adaptation; (v) climate change mitigation; and (vi) capacity building.

140. Third National Communication Report to the United Framework Convention on Climate Change Under the obligations agreed upon in the Paris Agreement, Tonga submitted its third national communication report (NCR) to the UNFCCC in 2015. The second national communication (SNC) report in 2012. Tonga's Third National Communication was then submitted in 2020.

The Vulnerability Assessment Report (VAR) is a key document that provides an overview of Tonga’s vulnerability to the impacts of climate change on vulnerable sectors including agriculture, fisheries, coastal areas, water resources, lands, infrastructure, disaster risk reduction and management. The VAR also identifies adaptation measures for each sector. The report highlights sea level rise, storm surges such as coastal erosion and hurricanes. While sea has prioritized high tide to the future integrated coastal zone management plan (ICZM) and climate change adaptation strategy (CCAS), it is important that Tonga continues to address climate change issues and disaster risks on the Environment Impact Assessment Process Conduct LIDAR survey to reduce vulnerability of communities in the coastal areas. The report also highlights the need for capacity building, awareness raising measures to vulnerable communities on the coast; and to provide coastal reinforcement and afforestation.

141. The six national development priorities are: (i) climate change adaptation; (ii) disaster risk reduction and management; (iii) poverty alleviation; (iv) economic diversification; (v) sustainable livelihoods; and (vi) infrastructure development. These six areas set out to policy objectives and targets and an implementation strategy for the country to achieve its vision of a Resilient Tonga by 2035 through specific environmental, social and economic targets ultimately aimed at increasing Tonga's resilience to climate change risk management. The six objectives are: (1) Mainstreaming for a resilient Tonga; (2) Implement a coordinated approach to reduce vulnerability and increase adaptive capacity of communities in the coastal areas; (3) Promote sustainable livelihoods, including building assets); (4) Finance; and (6) Regional and international cooperation. These build on Tonga’s first Joint National Action Plan (JNAP) for Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management.

142. Tonga's National Designated Authority (NDA), ensures that the proposal is consistent with Tonga national climate change adaptation strategy, disaster risk reduction and management plan and other relevant policies. The NDA also ensures that the JNAP Task Force provides recommendations to the NDA based on their screening of the concept.

143. It is important for Tonga to have a clear understanding of its vulnerability in order to develop appropriate strategies for better informed climate risk monitoring and coastal adaptation planning, providing key capacity building, training and mentoring for coastal communities. The UNDP has been working with the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Disaster Management for data collection and monitoring to progress Objective 2 of the JNAP.

144. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) in Tonga is known as the UNDP Office (hereafter “UNDP OTC” or “Country Office”) in the implementation of this project lies on its experience with effective facilitation of partnerships with other UN Agencies and international organizations, including the Pacific Community (SPC), the Secretariat of the Pacific Regional Environment Programme (SPREP) and the Council of Regional Organizations of the Pacific, such as SPCREP, SPGCOOP, SPCFIS and several NGOs.

UNDP has been working in Tonga for over 30 years. UNDP has supported this project in Tonga under the umbrella of the United Nations Development Assistance Framework. The UNDP national level support to Tonga is provided by the Pacific Office (PO) based in Suva, Fiji. The UNDP PO provides technical and financial assistance on Sub-Regional Environmental management, climate change and disaster risk management. In particular, “improved resilience of PICTs with emphasis on coastal areas” is one of the four thematic priorities under the Pacific Climate Change Adaptation/Disaster Risk Management (CCADRM) Sub-Regional Outcome 4.

Such support has been provided through a range of projects in Tonga and globally with a global and senior technical advisor. The global network of the regional based advisors enables sharing and dissemination of information, best practices and lessons learned on climate change adaptation and disaster risk management. This comparative advantage for GCF focal areas lies in its global networks of country offices –such as the UNDP Pacific Office (PO) in Fiji, which has a strong presence in Tonga. The UNDP PO is currently providing technical assistance to Tonga strengthening as well as promotion of NGO and community participation. This experience means that UNDP will be able to assist Tonga with its GCF project proposal. In addition, the UNDP PO in Fiji works with the GCF focal point.

146. UNDP's added value is also described as:

* Accountability: a track record of quality management of development work at all levels; M&E and learning processes.
* Experience: extensive experience in the design and implementation of experienced and qualified staff with expertise in climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (e.g., climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction) in country offices and regional headquarters,
* Regional and global cooperation: experience with developing synergies and cooperation at the regional and global levels to ensure that Tonga’s needs are met.
* Coordination with other UN agencies: a mandate to support coordination and collaboration between other UN agencies.
* UNDP has a proven ability to: i) formulate project proposals, ii) collaborate with development partners and donors in the design of projects, iii) provide technical assistance for project implementation, iv) provide training and capacity building support and further developing national/local capacities for implementation; and v) contribute to ongoing learning.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 53

## D

### Several rounds of discussions with stakeholders on adaptation priorities and options in Tongatapu, Ha'apai and Vavau inform the design proposed project.

The project builds on past community consultations and ongoing partnership of the Government of Tonga with UNDP, World Bank, ADB, SPC, NCD and EU.

### The first rounds of consultations were undertaken between 2017 and then again in 2022:

In north-eastern Tongatapu, Government has undertaken a series of consultations but most interest in February and March 2020. In Ha'apai, the first round was held on 3-4 December 2019. In Vavau, the first round was held on 5 May 2020. These earlier consultations placed a particular emphasis on seeking commitments from community members in continuous engagement in OAMs.

A series of workshops were held with key stakeholders including local government officials, representatives of District and Officer districts were held on 6-7 February 2017 all at the project target areas. In the consultations community members were asked to identify their adaptation priorities and options for addressing these. The views of women and men residents on the coastal protection measures were collected.

A final approval meeting was held in the capital on 5 March or 8 March, 2017.

### These earlier consultations were then further updated during 2023:

In August 2023 ongoing consultations were held to discuss the project design and implementation. The key stakeholders included women and men residents of Tongatapu, Ha'apai as with Tongatapu's Key CSOs (details of these consultations are included in Annex 7). Consultations were also held with local government officials and representatives of District and Officer districts allowing for both the post-cyclone context, and then clarifications to proposed designs (September-October 2023).

### The consultation process resulted particularly to the following outcomes that are considered essential for successful project design and implementation:

- Identification/identification of climate change priority actions as a result of participatory, gender-responsive consultations.
- Increased awareness among local communities, NGOs and local governmental about specific interventions and their importance.
- Preparing the local leaders and communities and seeking their commitment for their active participation in project implementation.
- A full understanding about women's specific needs and the specific roles they play in climate change adaptation at a household level.
- Acknowledging the leading role of the youth in monitoring activities and data collection.
- Obtaining commitments from key stakeholders to support the project and its proposed adaptation actions.
- Agreeing on the specific costs of government ministries, local governments, NGOs, and local communities in project implementation.

## 151. The project will reduce the vulnerability of critical coastal areas to erosion and inundation in Ha'apai (north-eastern Tongatapu) and increase long-term resilience of communities in Tongatapu, thereby contributing to enhanced national and community-level adaptation to climate change impacts on coastal zones.

The objectives will be achieved through the following considerations that have been reflected in the design of the project:

- Selection of tested, locally appropriate options, based on detailed site-specific assessments and public consultations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 54

### Integrated and capacity-building of technical ministries and local communities for ensuring their long-term engagement not only for monitoring of the GCF investments but also for replicating the project results beyond the project geographical boundaries.

### Mobilizing community support for implementing local land management and ecosystem restoration measures within the context of climate change adaptation in Tonga.

### A comprehensive approach to removal of multiple barriers within a single project framework.

The project will focus on the coastal zone of Nukufetau, Ha'apai, and Tongatapu islands. A successful example of (coral) rock revetment is built in the capital city of Nuku'alofa in Tonga which was constructed in 1983 following the storm surge from Cyclone Gita. The revetment consists of a series of concrete blocks laid horizontally and vertically, with some vertical. When the storm surge from Cyclone Gita resulted in sea overtopping the reef crest, parts of the sea wall were washed away. The revetment was built by the Government of Tonga with funding from Germany. Today, after more than 30 years of operation, it is still functioning well and has been recognized as a successful example.

The rock revetments built in the 1960s in the vicinity of Nukufetau and Nukutavake in north-eastern Tonga have been identified as examples of good practice. However, these revetments are not considered to be sustainable because they were constructed largely due to the lack of knowledge about the adjacent ADE-assisted sedimentation event that although only comprising 10% of the total volume of material deposited by Cyclone Gita, it caused a significant amount of erosion.

Alternative solutions have been considered in the design of this proposed project. Two alternatives to the project were considered: these are the general options of "retreat" and "accommodate". The former entails protection of possessions and infrastructure through relocation or abandonment; the latter involves adaptation measures such as rock revetments, seawalls, and other structures.

A cost-benefit analysis conducted in PASAP using data for Lituania, reveals that the retreat option consistently results in higher costs than the accommodation option. This is because the accommodation option can be implemented under a wide range of climate change scenarios. While the PASAP analysis recognizes that these options are not mutually exclusive, the benefit-cost ratio of a combined approach was found to be lower than for either alternative alone.

The option of "retreat" while demonstrating relative economic advantages, was considered by most community members as impractical and undesirable because it would require them to abandon their homes and possessions. The option of "accommodate", elevating houses would achieve only slowly as such a solution can be practically promoted as the existing houses become more and more damaged.

The project will focus on the coastal zone of Nukufetau, Ha'apai, and Tongatapu islands. A successful example of (coral) rock revetment is built in the capital city of Nuku'alofa in Tonga which was constructed in 1983 following the storm surge from Cyclone Gita.

### 154 ADB has also investigated multiple alternative options for protecting assets in the north-eastern coast of Tonga. The option of moving key infrastructure (e.g., road) inland was considered but rejected on the same grounds as land tenure issues and lack of space. The option of constructing seawalls and revetments was found to be technically feasible, but not economically viable.

157 The Government of Tonga has committed to financing the project through a loan from the Green Climate Fund (GCF), with co-financing from the World Bank and other donors.

158 The GCF will provide technical assistance for the project's implementation, including capacity building in areas such as climate change adaptation, disaster risk management, and sustainable development. This support is crucial to ensure that Tonga can effectively implement the project and achieve its goals of reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increasing resilience to climate change.

159 The project aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 30% compared to business-as-usual scenarios over a period of 20 years, with an additional 7% reduction in emissions from avoided deforestation. This target is consistent with Tonga's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) under the Paris Agreement and aligns with the global goal of limiting warming to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels.

160 The project will also contribute to Tonga's Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 7 on affordable and clean energy, SDG 9 on industry, innovation, and infrastructure, and SDG 13 on climate action. By reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increasing resilience to climate change, the project will help Tonga achieve these goals and contribute to global efforts to combat climate change.

161 The project is expected to generate significant co-benefits in terms of improved air quality, reduced health impacts from pollution, increased energy security, and enhanced disaster risk management. These benefits are particularly important for Tonga, which faces significant challenges related to climate change, including rising sea levels, more frequent extreme weather events, and increased vulnerability to natural disasters.

162 The project will be implemented in collaboration with the Government of Tonga and other stakeholders, including local communities, civil society organizations, and private sector entities. This collaborative approach is essential for ensuring that the project is tailored to meet the specific needs and priorities of Tonga's population and aligns with national development plans.

163 The project will be monitored using state-of-the-art technologies, such as remote sensing and satellite imagery, to track progress towards its goals and ensure that it remains on course. This monitoring will also help identify any potential challenges or issues early on and allow for timely adjustments to the project's implementation plan.

The proposed project will integrate best practices and lessons learned from SPREP and UNDP, two leading development organizations in the South Pacific Region. Consequently, the investments in this project will not only be replicated in other SPC countries but also internationally. These lessons will be shared widely through awareness campaigns as well as internationally to contribute to current knowledge on building climate resilience.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 58

## Replicability

Presently there is limited technical capacity in the region for planning and expertise and access to data to allow for the identification of land areas for planning purposes. This will be addressed through engagement with national governments, but not local or national stakeholders.

### Medium

Output 2 undertakes national capacity building in climate change adaptation and vulnerability assessment. At the same time, the Output builds national capacity at the sub-national level by mentoring and training key staff. Documentation of processes and lessons learned will be provided for future agreement. Though a national approach to climate change adaptation will also be of wider interest in the face of increasing sea levels, it is likely that other SIDS in the Pacific Islands region and elsewhere may wish to adopt this at the community level.

### Infrastructure protection

Infrastructure protection has been applied in the Pacific Islands region but with limited success. This is due to a lack of data on vulnerability and exposure, as well as the high cost of infrastructure replacement.

## Medium

The National Dialogue Platform will receive technical support in the form of workshops and training sessions in the face of sea level rise. This and the project will also provide support for national capacity building at all levels (national, sub-national) to ensure that communities are better prepared to adapt to climate change impacts.


---
**Page 63**
---

## Sustainability

Medium


The project aims to mainstream climate change considerations at all levels of government from a high level through to community level. This is a means to ensure that Tonga’s national development plans and policies are aligned with respect to adaptation planning.


The project will support and facilitate the National Strategic Platform and poverty cluster activities.


The project will provide critical data and information on climate change hazards, vulnerability and risk such as supporting coastal LGTM surveys and the identification of key hazard parameters. This also includes providing information on key climate change hazards, which also supports the development of a national adaptation plan.


The project will support and facilitate the National Strategic Platform and poverty cluster activities.


The project will provide critical data and information on climate change hazards, vulnerability and risk such as supporting coastal LGTM surveys and the identification of key hazard parameters. This also includes providing information on key climate change hazards, which also supports the development of a national adaptation plan.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Besides the arrangements (e.g., annual performance reports) laid out in Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), please give a summary of the project programme specific arrangements for monitoring, reporting and evaluation. The AMA requires that all projects report to the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser (RTA) on an annual basis. In addition, the Project Manager will be required to submit a mid-term progress report to the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office and the UNDP RTA as per the project programme specific arrangements. The AMA also requires that all projects report to the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser (RTA) on an annual basis.

173 Project level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP PSMR and the UNDP Evaluation Policy, and also in line with DCP Evaluation Policy and PRM. The primary responsibility for day-to-day project monitoring and implementation rests with the Project Manager. The Project Manager will develop annual work plans to ensure that all project activities are completed on time and within budget. The Project Manager will be responsible for ensuring that all project staff maintain a high level of transparency, responsibility and accountability in implementing project activities. The Project Manager will also ensure that all project staff maintain a high level of transparency, responsibility and accountability in monitoring and reporting project results.

174 An independent mid-term re-evaluation will be undertaken, and the findings and responsibilities outlined in the management response will be incorporated as recommendations for improvement into the final evaluation report. The mid-term re-evaluation will be carried out by the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser (RTA) on an annual basis.

175 An independent terminal evaluation (TE) will take place when project activities have concluded. The terms of reference, the evaluation process and the final TE report will be finalized in consultation with the Project Board. The final TE report will be carried out by the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser and will be approved by the Project Board. The TE report will be available in English.


---
**Page 79**
---

176\. The UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office will include the planned project evaluations in the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office evaluation plan, and will upload the evaluation report (in English) and the management response to the public UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre (ERC) <https://erc.undp.org>.

177\. The UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office will retain all M&E records for this project for up to seven years after project financial closure in order to support ex-post evaluations.

178\. A detailed M&E budget (1% of the total GCF grant), monitoring plan and evaluation plan will be included in the UNDP project document.

179\. An ex-post technical assessment will be carried out with respect to the coastal protection measures under output 3. Lessons learned across all Outputs will be documented, shared and disseminated through the Project Board.

180\. The UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office will support the Project Manager as needed, including through annual supervision missions. The UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office is responsible for complying with UNDP project level M&E requirements as outlined in the UNDP POPP. Additional M&E implementation quality assurance and troubleshooting will be provided by the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor.

181\. A final review of the UNDP project documentation has been signed off by all relevant parties to it: a) ex- current project stakeholders to the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that influence project implementation; b) discuss the roles and responsibilities of the project team, including reporting and communication protocols; c) discuss the project’s M&E framework and finalize the M&E plan; d) review financial reporting procedures and mandatory requirements, and agree on the arrangements for the annual audit (e) plan and schedule Project Board meetings and other key milestones. The Project Board will be chaired by the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office Director and approved by the Project Board and supported by the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor.

183\. A project implementation plan has been developed to ensure smooth implementation. The Project Manager, the UNDP Pacific Multi-Country Office, and the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor will provide objective input to the annual GCF APR. The Project Manager will ensure that the indicators included in the project results framework are monitored on a regular basis by the Project Team. Any changes or updates to the project implementation plan, including any new safeguards, updates, challenges and delays must also be monitored by the Project Manager and reported in the GCF APR. The GCF APR will be shared with the Project Board for review at each Project Board meeting.

## Selected Risk Factor 5
### Category: Technical and operational
#### Probability: High
#### Impact: Medium

**Description:** The national dialogue on climate-land use land does not lead to any agreements, especially from landowners, for the revision of the national land-use plan.

**Mitigation Measure(s):**
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. If the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurrence* if at what level?

This risk is recognized due to the lack of tangible change arising from past efforts in changing the land tenure architecture. The current land tenure architecture, which has been in place for decades and is deeply rooted in social norms and practices, makes it difficult to implement the actions, particularly those that involve voluntary relocation or leasing currently owned land, will be widely accepted – particularly by communities who have a vested interest in maintaining their traditional use of land. Even if agreements are reached within this architecture, though very limited in number, did not result in a tangible change. None the less, even if agreements are not reached within

## Selected Risk Factor 6
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Environmental | Low | High |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description
Financial risks may impact the implementation of project activities (e.g., delays, foreign exchange risk due to currency fluctuations).

### Mitigation Measures:
Please describe how the identified risk will not be mitigated or managed. The mitigation measures lower the probability of adverse impacts on Tonga's climate change adaptation efforts.

All standard procurement and financial risks have been considered in the design of the project and UNDP delivers oversight and quality assurance for all procurements. A contingency fund has been established to cover unforeseen costs, including those related to financing and budgeting that has been designed in consideration of inflation rates (forecasting to 2041+), current commodity exchange rates, and other financial risks.

## Selected Risk Factor 7
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Environmental | Low | High |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description
Extreme events, including cyclones, earthquakes, and tsunamis, may impact protection efforts under action 3.1 that could delay the implementation of the project through delays or damage during construction.

### Mitigation Measures:
Please describe how the identified risk will not be mitigated or managed. The mitigation measures lower the probability of adverse impacts on Tonga's climate change adaptation efforts.
There is a potential risk from extreme events (e.g., cyclones, earthquakes, and tsunamis), there is a potential risk during the project implementation period, including during the construction periods. Tonga has early warning systems after for natural disasters such as cyclones, earthquakes, and tsunamis. The risks associated with these events are considered in the design of the project and include measures to mitigate climate change hazards; the rock materials (for the rock revetment and road raising) are considered durable and robust, and potential landslides are mitigated by careful planning.

The Contracted Party undertaking commitment is obliged to undertake construction in a way that leaves the works least vulnerable to such events or disruptions during construction. The Contracted Party is also obliged to respond to weather warnings (e.g., by cyclone) and take appropriate action as required.

## Selected Risk Factor 8
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Environmental | Low | High |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description
Limited workforce/labor, equipment and construction material (rocks for revetment) available for construction in Ha'apai coast.

### Mitigation Measures:
Please describe how the identified risk will not be mitigated or managed. The mitigation measures lower the probability of adverse impacts on Tonga's climate change adaptation efforts.
There is a limited amount of workforce/labor, equipment and construction material (rocks for revetment) available in Tonga for the project implementation period. This is based on past similar work undertaken in the country by local companies under ADB’s CCRP projects. The availability of labor and equipment will be monitored during project implementation to ensure that sufficient resources are available to complete the construction activities as planned. Coordination with other large-scale construction projects that may be undertaken in the country.

**Page 83**
---

Selected Risk Factor 9

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sanctions | Low | Medium |

Description

Project engage with entities or individuals listed on UN sanctions

Mitigation Measure(s)

This project is implemented by UNDP under the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) and will be guided by UNDP’s policy framework. UNDP, by virtue of being an organ of the United Nations and therefore being part of the same entity, is also beholden to the principles on countering the financing of terrorism. UNDP systematically screens all entities listed on the Consolidated UN Security Council Sanctions List (https://www.un.org/securitycouncil/content/sc-consolidated-list). As part of its due diligence, UNDP Pacific Multi-Year Programmes (MYPs) will also screen all entities listed in the UN Security Council’s Financial Sanctions List (https://www.un.org/securitycouncil/content/sc-consolidated-list) prior to engaging with them for the project. Bidding documents will also include a statement that the bidder has not been subject to any sanctions imposed by the United Nations Security Council and is not on the Consolidated UN Security Council Sanctions List before engaging with them for the project.

While selecting partners and private sector entities, Capacity Assessments tools are also used to identify these entities and screen against the Consolidated UN Security Council Sanctions List.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ML/FT | Low | Medium |

Description

Money laundering and terrorist financing risks

Mitigation Measure(s)

This project is implemented by UNDP under the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) and will be guided by UNDP’s policy framework. UNDP, by virtue of being an organ of the United Nations and therefore being part of the same entity, is also beholden to the principles on countering the financing of terrorism. UNDP systematically screens all entities considered to be contracted by or partner with UNDP against the Consolidated UN Security Council Sanctions List (https://www.un.org/securitycouncil/content/sc-consolidated-list) for the latest information on sanctions. UNDP, through Lead Agencies and/or implementing partners, will also screen all entities listed in the UN Security Council’s Financial Sanctions List (https://www.un.org/securitycouncil/content/sc-consolidated-list) prior to engaging with them for the project and countering of Terror Finance. In cases where there is Positive Match, UNDP escalates such instances to Regional Bureau or Country Office for further action. UNDP will also screen all entities against the Financial Sanctions List (https://www.un.org/securitycouncil/content/sc-consolidated-list) prior to engaging with them for the project as well as Project Risk Register where status of risk treatment actions is regularly monitored, updated and reported.


Selected Risk Factor 11

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Prohibited practices | Low | Medium |

Description

Prohibited Practices Risks

Mitigation Measure(s)

UNDP applies a zero tolerance policy in relation to fraud and corruption. The guiding principles of UNDP’s commitment to prevent, identify and address acts of fraud and corruption are set out in the UNDP Policy Against Fraud and Corruption Practices (the “FCP Policy”), which applies to all activities and operations of UNDP, including projects and programmes. UNDP will ensure that its partners and private sector entities are also aware of these principles and policy frameworks in relation to procurement, financial management, internal control and accountability and staff rules and regulations.


---
**Page 84**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 80

## Under the terms of the FCP Policy and related policies, UNDP has a commitment, when developing a new programme or project, to ensure that risks related to fraud and corrupt practices are fully identified and considered in the preparation phase of such programmes/projects. This is done through the implementation of an appropriate process in place. In this light, the processes and requirements for UNDP's partnership capacity assessment (PCAT) require UNDP to carry out a due diligence exercise on each potential partner prior to entering into any formal agreement with that partner in relation to the potential partners who will be involved in the implementation of such programmes/projects. Such capacity assessments are conducted by UNDP's internal PCAT team and include an evaluation of the risk profile of the partner against the Consolidated UN Security Council Sanctions List (to which UNDP, by virtue of being an implementing agency for the United Nations, is subject to). The assessment also includes a review of any history of fraud, corruption or other fraudulent practices and/or potential conflicts of interest. Based on this capacity assessment, UNDP will determine whether there are adequate safeguards in place within the programme/project to prevent and address risks and acts of fraud, corruption or AML/CFT that may be identified.

UNDP's internal Partner Capacity Assessment Tool (PCAT) was applied assesses the capacities of MEDECIE, MUNIR and OASIS as potential partners to be engaged in the project. The assessment includes a questionnaire regarding a history of fraud, corruption, money laundering, financing terrorism and the like. For all three parties, the assessment results were satisfactory. In addition, an additional due diligence exercise was conducted by UNDP. This assessment was conducted for MEDECIE, the lead government agency for this project, and they were assessed for the existence of a risk management framework that includes the AML/CFT policies and the Policy Against Fraud and Corrupt Practices. Based on assessing the PCAT and HACT results, as well as the size of the proposed project, the Government and UNDP agreed to apply the enhanced due diligence process. This will be conducted by UNDP's internal PCAT team in accordance with a framework that includes the AML/CFT policies and the Policy Against Fraud and Corrupt Practices, will be applied during the implementation.

As part of its incident management procedures, UNDP also establishes project level grievance redress mechanism; which is an independent mechanism allows grievances from direct beneficiaries are duly reported and efforts are made to resolve the concerns. Communities / beneficiaries are also informed about OAI helpline to report incidences of fraud, corruption, prohibited practices and any wrong doing for direct reporting.

## Social Management Measures - The project has been designed with the assistance of stakeholders and aims to provide a framework for managing social issues during construction and operation activities, including land acquisition, involuntary resettlement or acquisition of land although there may be temporary impacts on land during construction activities. These measures are intended to ensure that all parties involved in the project are aware of their rights and responsibilities and can work together effectively to avoid or minimize conflicts. Community consultation is crucial, and the Stakeholder Engagement Strategy (see Annex 1) will guide how this process should take place. The project team will also engage with local communities during project design and implementation alike.

### Assessment of Cultural Heritage - Any natural heritage cultural buildings or monuments are known to exist in areas where the project will be undertaken. The Archaeological, Indigenous and Cultural Heritage monitoring program (see Annex 2) will ensure that any such sites are identified before construction begins. This will involve surveying site personnel (including contractors), identify and collect any cultural heritage items worthy of protection, consult with the relevant authorities to determine how these should be managed during project implementation, and monitor their condition during construction and will seek to work in the area where any human remains are discovered.

### Emergency Response - In case of an emergency that could result in serious health, safety, and environmental (catastrophic) damage, emergency response or contingency actions will be implemented as soon as possible to limit the extent of environmental damage.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately | page)

### Purpose
The purpose of this document is to provide a gender analysis for the project in line with the requirements of GOFC’s Gender Policy. Conform a gender assessment and action plan exists describing the process used to develop both the gender analysis and the action plan. The gender analysis should be based on an understanding of the key vulnerability identified by the gender assessment. Include if stakeholder consultations have taken place to develop the key vulnerability, and if so, provide details of these consultations. If the project is funded through a GOFC grant or funds from government, key gender related results to be expected from the project/programme with larger implementation period should also be included. If the project has been subject to an independent gender assessment, indicate what conclusions were achieved.

### Project Gender Assessment
The gender assessment and analysis undertaken during the design of this project (and updated during 2023), led to including gender considerations across project outputs and activities and will serve as the basis for gender-responsive implementation. The key vulnerability identified by the gender assessment is that women are disproportionately affected in terms of internal displacement, poverty, and lack of access to resources. This is due to a range of factors such as limited access to education, economic opportunities, and decision-making power.

The World Bank’s Gender Strategy 2014-2023 (Gender Strategy) provides the framework for GOFC’s gender strategy. The World Bank’s Gender Strategy 2014-2023 (Gender Strategy) provides the framework for GOFC’s gender strategy. The World Bank’s Gender Strategy 2014-2023 (Gender Strategy) provides the framework for GOFC’s gender strategy.

### Key Vulnerabilities
The key vulnerabilities in responding to extreme weather events faced by women and men, as well as other vulnerable groups; are: access to resources, access to information, access to decision-making power, and access to services. These vulnerabilities will be addressed through the following actions:

1. **Gender-sensitive project design:** The project team will ensure that gender is considered in all aspects of the project activities.
2. **Strengthening women’s access to, and control over, environmental resources and the goods/services that they provide into the design of ecosystem restoration and monitoring activities:**
3. **The project team will use a range disaggregated data during the project implementation so that gender- and age-responsive decisions can be supported:**

### Women and participation
Ensure equitable participation of women, men, youth and elderly people at community-level adaptation actions.

### 198. The financial management and procurement of this project will follow UNDP financial rules and regulations available here: https://info.undp.org/global/documents/fin/Financial Rules and Regulations_E.pdf Further guidance is outlined in the Procurement Plan.

#### 199. UNDP has comprehensive procurement policies in place as outlined in the “Contracts and Procurement” section of UNDP’s Procurement Policy Manual (PPM). These policies are designed to ensure that UNDP complies with all relevant international guidelines across each phase of this procurement process, and they apply to all procurements in UNDP. See here for more information: https://www.undp.org/content/undp/en/home/about/united-nations-universal-values-and-guidelines.html

#### The following principles: Best Value for Money; which consists of the selection of the offer that best meets the ends and needs of the project, while ensuring that the UNDP’s procurement policies are followed; Openness and Transparency; where competitive processes are fair, open, and non-biased. All potential vendors will be treated equally, and the process will be transparent to all parties involved in the procurement process; Fairness and Impartiality; which means that all parties have an equal opportunity to participate in the procurement process; and, Equal Opportunity for Participation; which ensures that all parties are given a fair chance to participate in the procurement process. This is consistent with the Universal Values of UNDP and the United Nations.

#### 200. For this project, no separate procurement arrangements for goods and services will be carried out by UNDP and UNDP procurement procedures will be applied (PPD), as outlined in the Procurement Plan. Additional details on the procurement process are provided below:

* The project will be implemented following the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) guidelines available here: https://www.undp.org/content/undp/en/home/about/united-nations-universal-values-and-guidelines.html
* UNDP will ensure that all procurements comply with UNDP’s procurement policies and procedures, informed by and targeted with any specific requirements agreed upon in the AMA.
* According to the current audit results of the implementing partner, the project will be implemented using the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) under UNDP assignment plans, based on the implementing partner's risk rating and UNDP guidelines. A scheduled audit will be conducted at least once a year by UNDP’s Independent Evaluation Office (IEO). The IEO will ensure that all procurements are in accordance with this plan work. A scheduled audit can consist of a financial audit and/or an internal control audit.
* UNDP’s Construction Works Policy will be applied to all construction works under this project.

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) specifies that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all documents submitted by applicants. However, certain categories of information contained within the funding programme proposal will not be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board. Such information may include, but is not limited to: financial details; personal data; and any other information that, in the opinion of the Board, would compromise the confidentiality of the project or the identity of the applicant. The presumption of disclosure does not apply where there is a reasonable expectation that disclosure will cause significant harm to an individual or entity. In such cases, the Board may determine on a case-by-case basis whether disclosure should be made and if so, under what conditions. This document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

NUKU’ALOFA, TONGA

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by the United Nations Development Programme regarding the Coastal Resilience Project in Tonga.

Dear Sir/Madam,

We refer to the project titled GCF Coastal Resilience Project in Tonga as included in the funding proposal submitted by the United Nations Development Programme to us on 8 November, 2021.

The undertaking hereby duly acknowledges receipt of the Ministry of Meteorology, Energy,
Information, Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change and Communications (MDEDCC), the National Designated Authority of Tonga.

Pursuant to GCF decision B/09/R.13, dated 8 November 2021, we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Government of Tonga has no objections to the project as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies, and plans of Tonga;
(c) In terms of environmental, social, economic, and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.


Yours Sincerely
