## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

* Climate change poses significant threats to Tonga, particularly through increased rainfall, cyclones, sea level rise and extreme sea level events, necessitating adaptation measures for people, infrastructure and coastal ecosystems. Tongatapu, Tonga’s most populous island, is a low-lying island with limited land available for expansion of existing settlements without adaptive interventions. Recognizing these challenges, a project proposed by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) aims to build long-term resilience on Tongatapu through immediate coastal protection and long-term adaptation strategies.

* The overall objective for the project is to build the long-term resilience of vulnerable coastal communities in Tonga’s most populous island, Tongatapu. This will be achieved by strengthening adaptive capacities through strategic infrastructure work, long-term climate risk informed adaptation planning and community-based disaster risk management interventions.


1. The project cost for the proposed project is USD 23,915,409 in grants, of which GCF investment is USD 22,656,499 in grants.
2. The project is expected to reach 100,178 people (100 per cent of the population in Tongatapu) and 3,000 households.


The environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category for this project is B (medium).


### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

* The project is structured in three components and activities as follows:

**Component 1: Strengthen knowledge, capacity and engagement for long-term adaptation planning supported through a multi-stakeholder engagement and dialogue platform (total cost: USD 4.71 million; GCF input: USD 4.08 million in grants)**


---
**Page 98**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
Scale: N/A

The overall objective of the project is to support the transformative adaptation agenda setting through a participatory process that builds policy consensus and promotes planning coherence across all levels of government, including coastal adaptation plans and land-use management processes.

The project will support 1,608 direct beneficiaries and 96,571 indirect beneficiaries, which together make up the entire population in Tonga. Half of all beneficiaries are women.

The project has already trained 24 officials from the Ministry of Environment on being trained in climate risk and adaptation for developing the community development plans and island-level spatial plans; 50 government officials receive training in incorporating climate risks into planning processes, including coastal adaptation; and 3,258 individuals living directly adjacent to the coastal resilience works under Output 3.

The project will support the development of community development plans and island-level spatial plans and the multi-stakeholder engagement platform, along with the strengthening of the climate data analysis and adaptation planning for coastal adaptation, will indirectly benefit Tongans by ensuring that the project impacts Tonga's entire population both directly and indirectly.


---
**Page 99**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

*This project will tackle a range of existing challenges in Tonga including complex land ownership and governance issues, difficulty to interpret climate risks and a lack of capacity for decision-making on future land-use planning with a climate perspective to fundamentally shift how Tonga addresses climate risks.* The project approach is to sea defence infrastructure in the coastal zone and to develop a national-level spatial adaptation plan and associated measures such as land-use planning.

*The project has the potential to scaling up and replication at the national and regional level through the development of spatial adaptation plans and policies that are widely accepted by the public and politicians, enhancing processes to collect and analyze critical data over time. The project will also contribute to the implementation of Tonga’s coastal zone management plans and build practical coastal protection measures using locally sourced materials along the coastal zones.*

*It also has high potential for knowledge-sharing and learning across multiple stakeholders under all components.*


3.3 Sustainable development potential

The project contributes to Sustainable Development Goals 13 (Climate action) and 11 (Sustainable cities and communities).

*The major social co-benefit is enhancing the overall well-being of the Tongan people. The project will reduce the negative impacts of climate change on livelihoods, including economic activities, education, public and medical services, and cultural events. Additionally, it includes a focus on improving water quality in coastal areas by reducing pollution from waterborne diseases and overburdening the public health facilities in Tonga. Implementation of the project will also contribute to the reduction of coastal inundation and water contamination, leading to improved public health in Tonga.*

*The main environmental co-benefit is restored biodiversity and reduced salinity levels in protected areas such as Tafuna Lagoon and Nukutapu. The cause of saline water intrusion into the fragile groundwater resources in Tongatapu, where groundwater is still used for drinking purposes, is a significant bacterial contamination by accelerating seepage from septic tanks and livestock pens.*

*The project also delivers gender co-benefits through a focus on ensuring strong participation of women in all aspects of planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and management processes, including developing long-term adaptation solutions through design thinking workshops, enhancing the capacity of women to shape local development and spatial planning.*


3.4 Needs of the recipient

Settlements in Tonga are concentrated in coastal areas and communities earn their livelihoods within one km of the coast. As a result, the livelihoods and critical infrastructure, such as hospitals, government buildings, schools and workplaces, located in the coastal zone are at risk from climate change impacts. The limited capacity for planning and development management capability of the government limits the long-term adaptation planning and infrastructure development in Tonga.

*In addition, the main capacity gap in construction works has been identified, namely poor attention to design, construction methodology and materials selection.* This project,


---
**Page 100**
---

therefore tends to address the key gap via a learning-by-doing approach, by seeking to use local suppliers and managing their work under competent coastal engineering supervision and undertaking a best practice work strategy that closely adheres to international standards.

26. Thus, GCF concessionality is expected to address the financial and technical-capacity barriers.


### 3.5 Country ownership
Scale: N/A

27. This project is included in Tonga's country programme.


#### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
Scale: N/A

28. The total project cost is USD 219.4 million with a GCF contribution of USD 22.6 million, benefiting the entire population of the country.

29. The economic analysis was carried out by comparing net present value with the availability of funds from the World Bank (as the development partner in Tonga) and economic internal rate of return. Given the nature of the public goods, the projects will not generate any cash flow and, therefore, financial internal rate of return is not calculated.

30. The economic analysis focuses on estimating the expected benefits to be delivered by Output 1 and Output 2. The costs associated with Outputs 1 and 2 are estimated and quantified, while accounting for the whole cost (the cost of all activities) of the project. The economic internal rate of return is estimated at 8.30 per cent, which is above the social discount rates of 6 per cent.

31. According to the International Monetary Fund 2023 Article IV Consultation, Tonga's high reliance on external financing and limited domestic resources make it difficult for the country to finance development spending. Further, under the fiscal year 2023 budget, the majority is expected to revert to a deficit. Tonga faces the dual challenge of addressing immediate needs while also investing in long-term sustainability for coastal adaptation and resilience measures.

32. Therefore, in this assessment, a selection of tested, locally appropriate and available options will be used, based on site-specific assessments and public consultations. In addition, strong local community participation and capacity-building will be promoted to ensure long-term sustainability for coastal adaptation planning and monitoring.


### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards


---
**Page 101**
---

### Project Background

The project aims to increase resilience of vulnerable coastal communities in the country through building resilient infrastructure and capacity on climate risk information and adaptation planning. The project will include the construction of 4km of physical coastal protection measures (coastal dunes) along the coast of Tongatapu (Ha'atafi) and soil erosion in the Northeastern Tongatapu (Hahake) and will be equipped with rock revetments preventing sedimentation, erosion and land loss. The project will also undertake activities that will strengthen national and local capacities for effective monitoring, maintenance and community adaptation actions with respect to monitoring and the status of ecosystems and coastal resources. These interventions are expected to contribute towards improved coastal and marine ecosystem services.

### Environmental and Social Risk Category and Safeguard Instrument

The project has been screened against the ADB's social and environmental screening process and deemed it equivalent to a category 2 project, which requires an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and/or resettlement and the activities will have potential risks/impacts that can be readily mitigated. An Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy (ESSP) is in place for ADB projects. The ESMP sets out the principles, rules, guidelines, and procedures for screening, assessing, and managing the potential social and environmental impacts of the interventions under the project. It also specifies how the project will comply with relevant laws and regulations, including those as to how these requirements will be met through the conduct of screening, assessment, approval, mitigation, monitoring, and reporting.

### Compliance with GCF's Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards

The discussions below describe how the project complies with the interim ESS GCF standards:

#### I. ES1: Avoidance of Significant Adverse Environmental Risks and Impacts

The main intention is that it will have potential adverse land use, environmental risks and impacts in Sub-Activity 3.1.2 (Construction of coastal protection measures in Ha'atafi). It also has the potential to cause significant adverse environmental impacts in the conduct of safeguards related activities during implementation.

#### II. ES2: Labor and Working Conditions

The construction of the coastal protection measure will be carried out by local workers who are employed on a daily basis, with no permanent employment or contract. Workers could be exposed to occupational health and safety risk particularly those who may be required to work in areas that have not been surveyed for hazards. Measures will be put in place to ensure compliance with relevant laws and regulations regarding occupational health and safety issues and will be provided with personal protective equipment.

For potential exposure to hazardous substances, the project will comply with the International Labour Organization's (ILO) Convention No 185 on Seafarers' Rights at Sea (SAR) including a sea exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) Action Plan will be established and implemented.

#### III. ES3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

Construction-related activities may result in generation of noise and vibration, dusts and sediments, effluent discharges to the sea, and sedimentation in water bodies. The construction of coastal protection measures could potentially lead to soil erosion and loss in soil productivity. However, wherever feasible, most of the excavated material will be reused on site for embankment construction. Other erosion control measures will also be employed to prevent sedimentation in the nearby marine habitats.

A water quality monitoring program will also be developed for the project.


---
**Page 102**
---

### GCF: Project No. 032 / ADD No. 018

#### a). ESS4. Community Health, Safety and Security:
Given the anticipated increase in construction-related works in the area, the community may also experience increased safety risks. For instance, there will be increased vehicular and machinery movements around and within the project site, which could lead to accidents or injuries among workers.

Project plans to mitigate this by fencing work areas to minimize public access, training drivers to observe proper driving techniques, and providing first aid kits at strategic locations for community activities. Continuous stakeholder engagement construction activity will also be limited during the daytime hours. Community management committees (CMCs) will also be formed to ensure that workers are aware of safety rules and procedures in place to prevent collapse that could result to accidents.

#### b). ESS5. Voluntary Resettlement:
The project is not expected to acquire any land and/or result to resettlement as the land where the project activities will be undertaken is owned by the government. Should there be economic displacement that may be encountered, it would be temporary in nature and limited to a few households living along the shoreline who could affect their livelihoods; the project has provided guidance to develop a livelihood plan for these households which ensures that their livelihood conditions are improved or at least restored.

#### c). ESS6. Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources:
The project is expected to have significant adverse direct impacts on flora and fauna in the target areas. Nevertheless, the installation of coastal protection infrastructure could potentially result in habitat destruction, degradation, fragmentation, displacement, entanglement and/or entrainment of benthic marine fauna and other coastal/marine organisms. Specific action that will be taken by the GCF to mitigate these adverse impacts include the controlled introduction of new invasive/woody species during construction activities. The project will also consult with relevant authorities such as the Fanga Upi Special Area Management Committee and the CMGs to be able to develop feasible mitigation measures for the area. The GCF also provide for the development of a Biodiversity Action Plan that can undertake biodiversity-related monitoring.

#### d). GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7 Indigenous Peoples:
Although there is no specific Indigenous People policy in place, the project is not expected to have any adverse impacts on indigenous peoples. Tangible measures will be taken under the AE and GCF standards as follows: People’s stakeholder engagement plan was developed during the project's design stage. It will guide all actions pertaining to the project that may affect indigenous people. The project will be implemented by a free, prior and informed consent protocol; to be developed together with local communities and Indigenous peoples. This will ensure that the project is carried out in accordance with the associated possible positive and negative consequences. They will be encouraged and given the time to express their views on how they would like to participate in the project and provide free, prior and informed consent. The protocol will then be applied to each activity of the project, as communities will be allowed to withdraw from any activity at any stage if they do not wish to provide free, prior and informed consent. In line with its roles and functions, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE, which is also encouraged to share emerging good practices and successful experiences.

#### e). ESS8. Cultural Heritage:
While there are no known cultural heritage sites, buildings or monuments in the project area, a cultural heritage impact assessment procedure will be developed as part of the construction stage ESMF and a cultural heritage training will be provided to all construction site personnel (including contractors).

The AE has conducted an initial SEAMR screening against a set of high-level indicators (at least per 2) to the environmental and social management framework) and identified moderate SEAMR risks of the proposed project given its regular interaction with local communities. The AE will continue to monitor and assess this situation work. Following the SEAMR risk screening by the AE, general mitigation measures have been

### Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The project is designed to support the Government of Tonga's efforts to enhance coastal resilience and provide medium-term vulnerability reduction measures in Tongatapu, while also contributing towards the overall aggregation capabilities of the government and the people of Tonga. As such, 3,609 people (of which 51% are women) will be directly impacted by this project, with potential vulnerability reduction and/or resilience-building activities in Tongatapu.

The project-specific gender assessment and gender action plan provide an overview of the context for gender equality in Tonga, including issues related to gender equity, climate change and the economy, which are relevant to this project context. It further identifies gender mainstreaming opportunities within the project design and implementation framework based on a desk review and stakeholder consultations with the Women's Affairs and Gender Equality Unit (WAGEU) of the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and Gender Equality. The plan also leverages on lessons learned and recommendations from past assessments undertaken by United Nations agencies, development partners, civil society organizations, etc. A range of


---
**Page 104**
---

gender roles, activities, outputs and targets have been presented and the impact statement for the gender action plan is "gender responsive and participatory adaptation solutions enhance immediate and medium-term resilience and long-term adaptive capacity of women and girls in Tonga". The PMU will be responsible for building the capacities and participatory spaces for women and girls to promote gender-responsive adaptation solutions.

Implementation of the gender action plan will be led by the AE UNDP, which will work in close collaboration with responsible parties, namely the government ministries and departments involved. The Women's Affairs and Gender Equality Division of the Ministry of Internal Affairs. The PMU will be based in Tonga and will work with an international gender specialist.

## Risks

### Project-specific execution risks

1. Macroeconomic risk: market factors (inflation and currency variation) may trigger inflation on costs of goods, services and labour that are essential to delivering the project activities. This could lead to cost overruns and delays in delivery of the project and/or increase project costs and risks in the funding proposal. The Secretariat will monitor budget utilisation through the PMU and will provide early warning if there is a risk of exceeding the approved budget.
2. Natural hazards: Tonga is prone to extreme events, including cyclones, earthquakes and tsunamis. These natural hazards may cause damage to infrastructure, loss of life, property damage, destruction or failure and result in delays and cost overruns. As part of the project design, the Secretariat will ensure that adequate insurance cover is provided for the contractor to be engaged, which entails the use of lump-sum contracts and requirements for relevant insurances and performance bonds, as well as the project budget, to include provision for contingencies.
3. Implementation risk due to limited amount of workforce/labour, equipment and construction materials available in Tonga: The Secretariat will ensure that the construction work required in Ha'atale case capacity limitation of the Government of Tonga to implement the specific activities of the project is mitigated by AE using the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM). This will be achieved through a phased approach to the implementation structure whereby the PMU will support the execution of project activities and will carry out quality assurance and monitoring. As part of the project design, the Secretariat will ensure that the AE has undertaken preliminary assessments in terms of availability and quality of construction materials and equipment required for the project. This will be reflected during the project approval and implementation phases, and the construction timeline allows for staggered delivery of construction materials and equipment.
4. Technical/implementation risk specific to Activity 3.1 (around 60% per cent of GCF financing): the construction work may not meet the required quality standards, leading to additional costs and delays in project completion. This is partly due to limited maintenance and safety issues partly owing to the lack of and/or limited experience of the AE in implementing such projects. To mitigate this risk for the construction phase at Ha'atale, there will be separate design and construction phases, which will be implemented by an experienced engineering consultancy firm and an experienced construction firm respectively. In addition, the term sheet of the AE will ensure that the detailed technical assessments and relevant studies are

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP234

## Proposal name: Tonga Coastal Resilience

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme

### Country: Tonga

### Project size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. The funding proposal for Tonga Coastal Resilience is for a small public sector adaptation project in environmental and social safeguards risk category II, to be implemented over a 7-year period and with an overall project lifespan of 20 years.

2. The Government of Tonga has requested the Government of Tonga through the Ministry of Meteorology, Energy, Information, Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change and Communications (MEDIECC) to submit this proposal under the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) approach, under which the project will be directly managed by UNDP. However, project implementation will also be co-located with the Government of Tonga [see para. 49 below]. The total cost of the project is USD $23 million, including a grant component of USD $18.5 million and a co-finance contribution of USD $2.12 million from the Government of Tonga.

3. Tackling climate change is a priority for the Government of Tonga. The aim of the Tonga Coastal Resilience project is to build the long-term resilience of vulnerable coastal communities to the direct impacts of climate change, including sea-level rise and increased storm surges, through platforms to achieve policy consensus, building national capability to manage and monitor climate-related risks, and strengthening community capacity for disaster risk reduction and resilience at the community, district and island level. This will be backed by engineering works to strengthen infrastructure in vulnerable coastal communities.

4. The project will focus on the largest and most populous islands of Tongatapu, which faces significant flooding risks and permanent losses from sea-level rise. Coastal flooding poses a risk to communities living along the coastlines of these islands, particularly in low-lying areas.

5. The risks of flooding are likely to increase with future sea-level rise, storm surges or intense rainfall, as coastal settlements are causing increased vulnerability for housing and infrastructure, and coastal land loss, with flow-on effects on livelihoods and food security.

### 1.1 Impact potential

**Scale: High**


---
**Page 109**
---

1. Tonga, a small island developing State (SIDS) located in the western South Pacific, comprises 175 small islands, many of which remain uninhabited. Tonga ranks as one of the most vulnerable country (out of 183) globally with respect to climate change impacts according to the Better Climate Change Index (BCCI). The BCCI is a composite index that measures Tonga’s extreme vulnerability to coastal inundation and erosion through climate-change, extreme weather events and natural disasters.
2. The country has experienced several major climate disasters in recent years, namely Tropical Cyclones (see 2014), (Gita) 2018 and Harold (2020). Located within the seismically active Pacific Ring of Fire, Tonga is also prone to volcanic eruptions and associated activities which trigger tsunamis as additional natural hazards. Recent non-climate-related disasters include the volcanic eruption in 2012, which generated tsunami waves up to 42 m.
3. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) has projected that climate change is leading to an increase in annual average atmospheric and sea surface temperatures of around 0.5°C per decade for Tonga as well as the entire region of the Pacific region. In response to questions from the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), the AE clarified the routine use of a long-term approach to sea level rise projections for SIDS with respect to climate change adaptation, using the IPCC’s AR5 scenario RCP 8.5, which estimates sea level rise at +60–87 cm and increases in temperature of around 2.6°C by 2100.
4. Pursuant to the TAP’s request, Tonga has conducted a national climate change adaptation assessment, which identifies Tonga's vulnerability to the adverse impacts of climate change and presents the country's exposure and vulnerability to coastal inundation affecting communities, infrastructure, agriculture, water resources, biodiversity and human health in coastal areas; water resources, lands, infrastructure, disaster risk management, biodiversity and human health. The assessment also highlights the vulnerability of the population and economy of the low-lying coastal areas in Tonga and the adaptation measures in place to counter the adverse impacts of climate change on these areas.
5. The funding proposal also refers to studies suggesting that significant displacement of communities could occur in the future. Tonga has a unique system of land ownership and management, which is based on customary law and practices.
6. Decision-making around land use and voluntary retreat as an option for long-term climate adaptation are complex issues with far-reaching implications for Tonga’s economy, resulting in ever greater strain on land resources in Tonga's capital city of Nuku'alofa and is increasing settlement in marginal, low-lying and high flood-exposure areas.
7. The funding proposal notes that the project will be carried out in a collaborative manner with several government departments, notably the Department of Climate Change and Environment, Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, Ministry of Education, Ministry of Infrastructure. Additionally, staff from other government departments and ministries including the Ministry of Internal Affairs and the Ministry of Finance, will also benefit from training.
8. The project will be delivered through three interrelated outputs as follows:
9. Output 1: Strengthened knowledge, capacity and engagement for incorporating climate risks into land use planning and management in Tonga through a multi-sectoral, multi-stakeholder engagement and dialogue platform;


---
**Page 110**
---

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

The proposals support Tonga in addressing climate change risks by combining long-term adaptation planning with short- to medium-term measures for minimizing the impacts of sea-level rise and related coastal hazards on human settlements and infrastructure.

### The project starts from a low baseline in Tonga and aims to tackle several hurdles, including:

* **Limited availability:** Insufficient quality of data and limited use planning at the national and community level.
* **Inadequate capacity:** Limited capacity for a functioning climate change adaptation framework. National-level adaptation is managed in Tonga at the focus of sea-level rise and related climate hazards.

The national-level adaptation framework does not currently include measures to address climate-sensitive issues such as those related to land-use planning and voluntary retreat (people or infrastructure) due to sea level rise and coastal inundation.

While some communities have developed more-completed community development plans (CDPs), these do not currently include measures to address climate risks. The proposal leverages support for participatory bottom-up planning processes, guidance, and capacity building, enabling communities to integrate climate adaptation into CDPs. However,


---
**Page 111**
---

The success of this approach and its scaling up will largely depend on Tonga’s success in securing additional funding for implementing these climate activities with COPs.

26. The high input of high-quality data for considering sea level rise or wave impacts is critical to designing and informing adaptation decisions at the national, island and community level. Strengthening the capacity of local communities to collect and use this type of high quality data on climate and coastal dynamics is also fundamental to achieving a paradigm shift.

27. The proposal refers to Tonga’s weak institutional capacity and highlights the challenges with cross-sectoral collaboration in implementing adaptation measures at scale. However, the proposal lacks specificity on the extent of involvement of each of these key agencies in the project implementation process and how the inter-agency coordination mechanisms will work in practice during implementation. This is an important aspect that could affect sustainability.

28. Access to technology – such as LiDAR (Light Detection and Ranging) equipment - would also become a key enabler for local authorities to build internal capacity and knowledge in mapping, monitoring and managing coastal resources through the collection of high quality data. Enhanced capacities for coastal management could potentially lead to transformational change in Tonga’s approach to coastal resource management. However, this capacity will only be built and sustained as part of project sustainability; data would be housed within the regional database and not locally. This is a concern given the limited local capacity and the mandate for data archiving and analysis services for the Pacific Islands.

29. The proposed approach to involve local suppliers alongside international technical experts, could provide an opportunity to build local capacity in terms of design and ongoing capacity beyond the project. The project also envisages drawing on voluntary services of local community members to assist with the construction of the revetment structure. This would help for the long-term functioning and visual monitoring of the revetment structure, especially after cyclone events, without which the long-term costs for these structures would be higher.

30. The project proposes a range of innovative approaches that could provide solutions for addressing coastal vulnerability, namely retreat, accommodate and protect. Drawing on international best practice in this area is important to ensure that the project designs work with natural processes. These models are site-specific, but emerging best practices could be replicated across other vulnerable coastal areas in Tonga and offer broader learning for the Pacific region.

31. In response to a question from the TFAP, the AE clarified that the project considers two potential locations for the rock revetment: (i) the existing location of the rock revetment solutions without taking into consideration that long-term flooding in lagoon and other low-lying areas in Tonga is unavoidable; and (ii) the second to unchecked coastal development based on a false assumption that the construction of the rock revetment would protect the area. The latter would be managed through activities under Outputs 1 and 2 to strengthen informed planning and decision-making.

36. The funding proposal proposes to use compatible design specifications based on a 40-year design life for the rock revetments. The environmental effects of building the revetment works are not considered in this proposal, which is a significant omission given that the project seeks by incorporating hard engineering interventions with coral restoration, mangrove planting and other soft engineering measures to reduce erosion. These interventions may face some transient issues arising from the construction phase, mainly through mobilization

# Sustainable development potential

The proposal highlights the project’s contribution to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and its direct contribution to SDG 13 (Climate action). It also notes the project's direct contribution to SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities).

The proposal has adequately highlighted the co-benefits and potential risks, with more details on the environmental and social co-benefits. The gender assessment and gender action plan (annex B) make up part of this document.

Environmental co-benefits: The project has been subjected to an environmental and social screening process in accordance with the IFC Performance Standards. The project does not require any land acquisition and/or resettlement, and all project activities will take place on land owned by the Crown.

Annex A also highlights some wider benefits arising from improved coastal protection works (Output 3), including increased biodiversity, reduced salinity levels in groundwater, and enhanced water quality. This is expected to improve the livelihoods of communities living near the sea for their livelihoods. Additionally, improved data access under Output 2 could influence the rhythm and use of information and data in policies, planning and actions at the national, subnational and community level.

Annex A also highlights some potential risks from the installation of coastal protection infrastructure. These include increased sedimentation rates due to changes in hydrodynamic processes; potential increases in erosion, entrainment, impingement and/or entrapment of marine organisms, including in or around protected areas; and damage to the environment and biodiversity as a result of construction activities. Mitigation measures are also included.

The project can provide co-benefits: The proposal can bring benefits to the country through the multi-stakeholder dialogue platform that could inform national policies, and directly to communities through the coastal protection and the strengthening of local-level planning to integrate climate change adaptation into development plans. This will help reduce erosion and wave overtopping, reduce flooding and landlocking, thereby bringing health benefits as well as economic benefits. The project also provides co-benefits for coastal benefit communities as it is also used as an evacuation route.

There is growing recognition in Tonga of the need for women’s political participation and leadership to achieve gender equality. Under the Women’s Equality Treaty and Strategic Action Plan (2019-2025) includes ‘Increased women’s leadership and equitable political representation’. In line with this, the project will contribute towards increasing coastal resilience; the proposal highlights three areas of benefit: engagement and participation in


---
**Page 113**
---

decision-making processes related to coastal resilience (including infrastructure development), livelihoods, and early warning and disaster risk reduction. The proposal indicates that gender will be mainstreamed across all activities, with measures to ensure representation and participation of women in decision-making at all levels.

The gender assessment notes that the CDP process has been instrumental in expanding women's participation in decision-making, and this approach will continue to inform project activities.

Women are also a key focus for the project. The proposal refers to UN Women's work on improving organizational status for Indigenous Peoples, the proposal refers to UNDP standards to outline potential risks that could be faced by women and girls, including gender-based violence, in relation to communities and youth. The proposal is accompanied by a gender action plan and a stakeholder engagement plan that will guide engagement and consultation across stakeholder groups.

Human Resources: The project proposes to hire 100 short-term jobs (35 male/15 female), particularly for the delivery of construction works under Output 3. It also notes that women are underrepresented in Tonga's public sector, with only 24% female representation. However, contractors may have limited capacities or not pay adequate attention to risks to vulnerable populations. In the short to medium term, the improved climate resilience of community infrastructure and assets could help to enhance economic services and operations.

The sustainable development potential of the project is assessed as high.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

Tonga has one of the world's smallest economies, as measured by gross domestic product (GDP). Its economy is highly dependent on remittances and climate-sensitive sectors such as agriculture and fishing. The country also faces a significant vulnerability to natural disasters, with an increasing burden of debt repayments and servicing. The country is highly reliant on donor funds, which are mainly used for infrastructure development projects. Tonga's economic resilience is also continuing to recover from the impacts of the coronavirus disease 2019 pandemic.

Tonga remains vulnerable to climate and non-climate shocks that put a strain on its already stretched resources. The country has made progress in developing climate-resilient infrastructure. The Global Climate Risk Index 2020 cited Tonga as one of the countries experiencing the highest levels of climate-related loss as a percentage of gross domestic product over 1999–2018.

Although the incidence of absolute poverty is low in Tonga, many experience economic hardship and vulnerability to shocks. The World Bank (2021) notes that approximately half of the population is considered poor owing to low living standards, with those in rural areas disproportionately affected by climate change. Tonga has been able to respond effectively to climate change and disaster events, despite receiving early warning information almost on a par with many APEC countries.

The project proposes to mainstream gender equality into all aspects of the project. The projects tended to be gender blind or insufficiently gender sensitive as compared with projects concerned with provision of social services. Land ownership-titling issues are also concentrated in rural areas, where women have limited access and control over land. Tonga has committed to developing strategic land-use planning.

The population of Tongatapu is projected to grow over the next decade, partly as a result of migration from other islands. This growth will put pressure on resources and climate change. This is likely to put more pressure on land resources in the capital city of Nuku'alofa leading to


---
**Page 114**
---

changes in land use and increasing patterns of settlement into marginal, low-lying and highly flood-exposed areas. Hence any development will require a long-term perspective that takes into account high risk areas that are prone to sea level rise and climate change induced flood hazard.

Despite having policies in place, government ministries lack adequate technical capacities to collect, analyse and use climate risk data, which limits their ability to mainstream climate risks into development planning processes. Consequently, most development decisions are taken on an ad hoc basis and often guided by budget availability rather than technical considerations. There is a need for better coordination and sharing of climate risk information between key government ministries.

Since 2012 Tonga has been promoting a bottom-up development planning process to help improve the resilience of communities in rural areas, where most people live. Many rural communities have developed CDPs which then feed into district development plans and upward into national strategic plans. These CDPs are intended to mainstream climate change and disaster risk management, as well as gender and social inclusion; however, this is yet to be carried out. Local communities lack capacities to undertake climate vulnerability assessments and do not have the capacity to implement climate-responsive and climate risk informed solutions and access adequate resources.

Needs of the recipient is assessed as high.


### 1.5 Country ownership

The proposal was prepared in consultation with key stakeholders and is accompanied by a no objection letter from the national designating authority of Tonga (items one) and UNDP (item D). The project aligns to the National Strategic Development Framework (2015-2025).

The proposal is aligned with and contributes to Tonga's national policy priorities, including the National Climate Change Action Plan 2016-2030, the country’s national strategic development framework (2015–2025). The proposal clearly outlines the relevant policies and plans, particularly noting the country’s national Climate Change Policy (2016), which sets out Tonga's commitment to climate change adaptation and mitigation. It also outlines the project's potential contribution to the Joint National Action Plan 3 on Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation (2015-2020). The proposal includes a detailed climate change adaptation plan, and sets out six policy objectives and targets and an implementation strategy for each of these. The proposed activities are consistent with Tonga’s commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, as outlined in its third national communication and its second nationally determined contribution (both submitted in 2020).

Proposed partners include government agencies such as MIEBEC, the Ministry of Internal Affairs (Women's Affairs and Gender Equality Division), MoGDR and the Prime Minister’s Office (Department of Climate Change). Consultations were also undertaken with community-based key civil society organizations and international development partners. The proposal notes that representation of women and youth was ensured in all consultations. For example, 100% of the women and youth who participated in the coastal protection measures were collected (see annex 7a). The funding proposal includes a stakeholder engagement plan (see annex 7b) as a basis for identifying, engaging and

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium to high

The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 23,919,500, comprising a request for GCF grant funding of USD 20,864,700 and an additional contribution from the Government of Tonga. Additionally, the government has agreed to co-finance USD 225,000 for operations and maintenance costs of coastal defense measures during project implementation with a further commitment to provide USD 139,000 in cash contributions (approximately 6% of the total cost, or just over 15% of the funding proposal). The funding proposal will also establish community volunteers for monitoring and maintenance of coastal defenses.

The economic analysis (annexes 3a and 3b) notes very high confidence that the project is economically efficient. The analysis suggests that costs should be maintained at the levels presented in the annexes.

The ITAP recognizes that lessons learned from the implementation of this project are likely to be applicable to other coastal protection projects in Tonga, and by year 7 of the project, UNDP will contribute USD 63,000 towards an international/regional learning event on climate-resilient coastal protection and management.


---
**Page 116**
---

### The presentation of the budget could be made more consistent to aid its readability across the funding proposal and the relevant annexes.

The detailed budget (annex 4) allocated the total project cost of USD 23,916,699 as follows: Output 1: USD 4,210,594; Output 2: USD 7,511,809; Output 3: USD 1,000,000; Output 4: USD 1,000,000. The monitoring and evaluation costs of USD 482,832 and USD 66,000 respectively are embedded within the outputs and are also separately reported in annexes 1, 7. The All fee request of USD 1,585,949 (as per annex 12) is included for reporting and formation.

#### The monitoring and evaluation (M&E) plan is not detailed enough. While the funding proposal outlines the project’s M&E framework, it does not detail the overall approach to track results and evidence of a paradigm shift. Information on M&E (annexes 11) is limited to listing data collection tools with individual outputs. The annexes do not provide information about how these indicators are linked to other indicators, how this links with the overall theory of change and the logical framework indicators.

No baseline or target values are currently provided for core indicator 3, “Value of physical assets more resilient to the effects of climate change,” in section E.3 of the logical framework. It has been noted that there is a need to develop baseline data on the different types of assets, such as crops and public assets such as communications towers, groundwater wells, etc., for each output. The development of these baseline values will be done as part of the project implementation and will be completed and reported before the end of the project.

The ITAP appreciates that the project proposes to build on lessons and synergies with other donor-funded projects. The financial analysis (annex 3) clearly explains the value addition of this GCF project in terms of its contribution towards achieving the SDGs, as well as its public good nature. The hard infrastructure under Output 3 falls a critical gap in seaward defense against climate change and is an important component of disaster risk reduction by Asian Development Bank resources. Infrastructure funded by GCF will replicate and build on this design and construction approach to ensure that it is more resilient than existing, demonstrated protection and prevented damage from the 2012 volcanic eruption and resultant tsunami referred to in paragraph 6 above.

The efficiency and effectiveness potential are assessed as medium to high.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### The ITAP considers that the proposal is sound, and recommends that the AE undertake the following:

#### By project mid-term, clarify what additional avenues for funding could become available to finance the climate and disaster risk activities that will be integrated within COPs to ensure their sustainability.

During project inception, clarify the roles, entry points and involvement of the different government ministries across implementation of all outputs (see para. 21 above).

Consider whether the Project Board could potentially be chaired by a senior official from MEDIEBC to ensure a joined-up approach in terms of climate change and disaster risk management implementation (see para. 51 above); and

Survey the project team members during the project inception period, including the establishment of the baseline and target value for core indicator 3 at mid-term (see para. 57 and 58 above).

### Impact potential

We appreciate the detailed analysis and considerations of the ITAP with regards to the project's impact potential and its High rating.

On the use of RCP8.5 and an expectation of reaching temperature-of-2.6°C by 2100, we note that this is a very high scenario (at this time) that it also is unlikely to achieve RCP5 across all indicators, such as global mean temperature increase. However, with regard to the sea level rise projections, we appreciate that the project has been designed to withstand the worst-case scenarios using RCP8.5 for Sea Level Rise (SLR) projections and will not be significantly impacted by SLR as a result of ice cap melting and Antarctic Ice sheet instability. The best advice available on coastal adaptation is therefore to use the pessimistic SLR projections from RCP8.5, as considered in this proposal, rather than the “optimistic” approach.

### Paradigm shift potential

The Medium to High rating is noted:

We appreciate the ITAP's acknowledgment of how the paradigm shift has been outlined in the Theory of Change as it is a key pillar of strength of the project design. Moreover, we appreciate that the project will be able to provide evidence-based recommendations on coastal vulnerability that has been built into the design of the project, namely, coastal resilience.

We note the importance of detailing the involvement of key Government agencies during project implementation to address challenges associated with weak institutional capacity and cross-governmental coordination. The project will also include a detailed engagement plan and processes of engaging key project stakeholders which will include Government agencies. This process is supported by the Project Management Unit (PMU) and will be provided support from the Project Management Unit personnel. The agreement on the involvement of Government agencies will thereby be responsive to capacities, roles and responsibilities, and have the potential to strengthen the institutional capacity building component of this process.

We recognize the important role the CDPs play in incorporating climate risks and adaptation measures, and the project will promote this by developing guidelines for mainstreaming climate change concerns into the CDPs. The importance of the long-term financing options is also noted as a key pillar of strength of the project design. We will continue to work with clarity on the mid-term the potential avenues available to finance the climate and disaster risk activities that will be integrated within CDPs.

# Introduction

The proposed GCF project is designed to support the Government of Tonga’s efforts to enhance coastal resilience and provide medium-term vulnerability reduction and adaptation benefits in the Ha'apai region as part of the Coastal Resilience Project (CRP) for the Kingdom of Tonga. The direct beneficiaries will be 39,417 people (of which 19,782 are women) who will benefit from activities in vulnerability reduction and/or resilience building activities in Tongatapu. Additionally, 100,179 people (of which 56,000 are women) living in the Ha'apai region will be indirectly affected by the project and are expected to benefit from strengthened adaptation capabilities of the government.

This Gender Assessment and Gender Action is prepared as part of the Coastal Resilience Project proposal for the Kingdom of Tonga. The assessment provides a gender analysis of the CRP for the Kingdom of Tonga and highlights the intersectionality between gender equality, climate change, and economic development in Tonga. It also identifies gender mainstreaming opportunities within the project context. The Gender Action Plan includes specific gender-mainstreaming activities across the three output areas, as well as the gender equality results that will be measured under each proposed project.

This gender assessment is based on:
- A desk review of relevant national policy documents, including the Tonga Strategic Development Framework 2015–2025 (TSDF), the National Action Plan for Gender Equality in Tonga (WEGI) and Policy and Strategic Plan of Action 2019–2025.
- The Joint National Adaptation Plan on Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management (JNAP) 2018–2030.
- Corporate strategies (2017–2021) of all the ministries of the government of Tonga.
- Community Development Plans of Ha'apai region, developed by the community in collaboration with the Prime Minister's Office and Mainstreaming of Rural Development Knowledge Centre (RDKC).
- Lessons learned and recommendations from past assessments and studies on gender equality undertaken by the Government of Tonga, UN agencies, development partners, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders. These are available from programs and projects currently being implemented.
- Stakeholder consultations with the Ministry of Women's Affairs and Gender Equality Division (WAGED), Ministry of Internal Affairs, and Civil Society Organizations.

## Gender equality and social inclusion in Tonga

The Tongan society revolves around four core cultural values: fale/heritage respect; tanovalai (respect for elders); fa'afetai (respect for women); and fa'amalolo (respect for men). Women have held high social status due to the fale system. The "fale" or the elder sister (or another chosen sister) holds a place of privilege and plays an important role in family decision-making. At the same time, the traditional roles of women are still very strong, with women being responsible for household chores and nurturing. In modern times, though Tongan women take part in all aspects of the society and meet government policies regarding fale tongian polities and society, they continue to be limited by antiquated legal frameworks and practices.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

At present, Tonga is facing a demographic shift due to net migration along with increasing vulnerability to climate change and disaster risks. The annual population growth rate is negative (0.1 percent) (-0.1). Nearly four out of five households (82.1 percent) are headed by women. This demographic change makes it imperative for Tonga to address gender equality in the context of climate change adaptation and development of the country.

Added to this, Tonga's vulnerability of the country to climate change and disaster risk is increasing. The overall score of Tonga on the ND-GAIN Index is 41.1 out of 100 (ranked 145 out of 185 countries). The lower the score the more vulnerable the country is to climate change and other global challenges, and less resilient it is in terms of its ability to adapt. Tonga is ranked as one of the most disaster-prone and most vulnerable country out of 385 measured counties. On the 2011 Global Climate Risk Index, Tonga ranks 7th out of 116 countries by average yearly weather-related loss for the period 2000-2013.

The disproportionate impact of these changes on women and other vulnerable groups is not yet recognized. In Tonga, there is a need to understand and unpack the intersections of gender inequality with a range of other dimensions of vulnerability and resilience that can be amplified by climate change.

The government recognizes the importance of equal political and economic participation of men and women for resilient development and has prioritized achievement of gender equality as its 2015 Gender and Social Inclusion (GSI) Strategy. The GSI Strategy is underpinned by the National Strategic Action Plan-2 on Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management (2018-2030). However, gaps remain in implementing these strategies to ensure that women's voices are heard and gender equality is promoted where women are being in the country.

## Changing demography

The population of Tonga is largely homogeneous. 98 percent of the population is ethnic Tongans, and the remainder is comprised of immigrants from various countries, including China and other Pacific Island Countries.

According to the 2011 Census, the number of women in Tonga is higher than the number of men (53.4% vs. 46.6%) as a percentage of the population, and this trend will continue over the next 66 years of the population. The number of males for every 100 females fell from 99 in 2016 to 95 males in 2021.

Largely due to emigration, the median age is now 34 and is projected to increase further. Tonga has an estimated fertility rate of four for every 100 working-aged persons; there are 83 females per 100 males. The median age of the population is 22 years, and 35 percent of the population is aged less than 15 years old. The median age of the population in Tonga is projected to increase further over time.

Despite a young population, the annual population growth rate remains negative due to emigration of working-age population.

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Migration and gender inequality

There are several interventional indices that have been developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) developed the Human Development Index (HDI), which measures life expectancy, education, and income per capita. In addition, UNDP also uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII). Gender Development Index (GDI) as well iniquity-adjusted Human Development Index (IHDI) to measure inequality. The GII is a composite index that measures gender gaps across three dimensions: health (specifically reproductive health), empowerment (specifically access to education and political participation), and income (specifically the ratio of female to male earned income). Gender development due to inequality between female and male achievements in these dimensions.

The GDI measures difference in male and female achievements in three basic dimensions of human development: life expectancy, education, and income. The GII is a composite index that measures gender gaps across the HDI values. Inequality reduction is measured by subtracting the GII from 100. A score equals the IHDI when there is no inequality across people but falls below the HDI value as inequalities increase.

Tonga has a GII of 0.631 in 2021 and ranks 160th out of 170 – which means that Tonga has high levels of gender inequality, with women having lower life expectancy than men (by an average of 8 years), a score of 0.965, which shows high equality in Human Development Index achievements between women and men. Overall, Tonga scored 0.743 on the HDI in 2021 and ranks 91 out of 131. HDI value for female is 0.728 and for male it’s 0.764. In other words, Tonga has high human development with little difference between women and men.

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Tonga Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

### Between male and female achievements, women facing better in the expectancy (17.3 years compared to 60.4 years for men), and in education attainment (mean of schooling is 11.5 years for women, and only 9.8 years for men). Issues where empowerment and economic participation are concerned, gender gaps remain in Tonga. Women earn on average $27,300 compared to a mean salary of $4,842 for women and $8,845 for men based on 2017 Purchasing Power Parity.² Tonga ranks 62 out of 156 countries in the World Bank’s Ease of Doing Business Index, and 93rd out of 144 countries on the HDI score. The human development level of Tonga is 10.6 percent from HDI to HDI.

At the same time, women tend to have higher perception of better life compared to men (91 and 88 percentage, respectively).³

### Poverty²

Tonga has succeeded in virtually eradicating extreme poverty (SDG 1.1), which is defined as people living on less than $1.00 per day. Poverty rates in Tonga have been steadily declining. In terms of monetary poverty, the poverty rate for Tongans living below the national poverty line was 25 percent in 2013. The National Poverty Line (which is around $8.60 per day) food poverty is negligible in the country. Tonga sits on a high income economy.

Poverty in Tonga is also highly spatially autocorrelated – meaning that the prevalence of poverty is geographically clustered and there is a major geographic difference in the extent of poverty across Tonga. The national poverty rate for Tongans living below the National Poverty Line was 25 percent in 2013. Tonga’s poor live in Tongatapu rural areas. Tongatapu is home to 74 percent of Tonga’s population. However, only 68 percent of Tongatapu residents are employed compared with 90 percent in urban areas and 95 percent in rural areas.

26 percent of the households are female headed households. At national level, poverty rate is lower for people living in female-headed households (14.3 percent) than male-headed households (14.8 percent). In Tongatapu, female headed households’ poverty rate is 14.5 percent, while it five percent points higher for male-headed households. However, in the outer islands (except Vava'u), female headed households are more likely to be poor compared with their rural counterparts. Vava'u poverty rate of female headed households is 36.4 percent whereas for male headed households is 28.5 percent.

Young people are more frequently affected by poverty. The highest poverty rates are found among the youngest age group (0 to 10 years old) with a rate of around 23 percent, while it is less than 20 percent for those aged between 11 and 19 years old. Poverty is also high among young children under ten who account for 47 percent of the population and 53 percent of the poor.

Multi-dimensional poverty refers to various deprivations experienced by poor people in their daily lives as a result of low income, lack of access to basic services, and limited opportunities. It was measured through a survey developed using the Comensual Approach which identified 29 items (range of things, activities, or conditions) that are considered important for Tongans’ well-being. The 29 items were grouped into four categories: 10 for adult population, 13 for children, and 5 for household items. The survey allows for classifying the population facing multi-dimensional poverty into four groups: the poor, not poor (the middle class), the vulnerable due to low income standards (i.e., low living standards but high income), and the vulnerable due to low living standards (i.e., low living standards but high income).

## Political Participation

Women were granted voting rights following the amendment of the constitution during the reign of Queen Salote Tupou III in 1951. However, women representation in the national legislature is well below a fair share. At present, there are only two female MPs (one elected in 2022 by-elections and one appointed) in the Parliament. Women's representation in the Tongan parliament is about 7 percent which is well below the global average of 26 percent.

# Annex 8 - Gender Segregation and Action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

### Table: Women’s political participation

| National level (2015) | Local government level (2015) |
|---|---|
| Women's representation | 7.6 percent |
| Women's representation | 3.9 percent |

Tongan Women's Coalition, a nongovernmental organization, has established a reserve seat for women in Parliament and made a submission to cabinet in 2017.

At the sub-national level, women’s representation though remains low, showing signs of improvement with the election of 5 women mayors (out of 34 mayors) and 6 women district officers who are elected every three years. In 2021, 1 woman district officer, and 6 women town officers were elected, which translates to 3 percent representation at this level.

This is considerable improvement from the 2018 local elections where only one woman district officer and one woman town officer were elected (about 1.1 percent of seats in local government).

However, it is important to note that none of these seven local government officials elected were from the main island of Tongatapu which has a much higher population live. More research is needed to unpack the 2023 local election results. But a 2021 survey points continued negative attitudes towards women in leadership roles and suggests that women are less likely than men to be considered more likely to have the “right” skills and experience for parliament.

Decision-making processes continue to be firmly male driven. Access to spaces (such as kava bars) is restricted for women. These spaces are often where actual decisions are made and restricted access of women to these spaces limits their ability to fully engage in decision-making.


### Voter’s Perceptions of Women as Leaders in Tonga

- 63 percent of respondents considered that the traditional role of a woman was “hierarchical, with men at the top of the hierarchy and therefore at the head of the Tongan family.”
- 69 percent of respondents considered that mothers/women should stay at home while children are being raised.
- 58 percent considered it appropriate if the father/men should go work while the working mother stays at home to raise her children.
- 80 percent of respondents felt that a woman, staying in her husband’s village, could participate in village meetings and decision-making.
- 65 percent of respondents considered that it was inappropriate for a mother to advise her husband to allow his wife to work outside the home.
- 52 percent of respondents considered that it was appropriate for a Tongan mother to be a wage earner while her husband is working at home.
- 66 percent believed that fathers and mothers should have equal access to financial income.
- 40 percent felt that women should not participate in village meetings and decision-making.
- 57 percent believed that men should lead in the village while 57 percent believed that both males and females could lead in the workplace.
- 31 percent of respondents felt that women and could work in parliament, but both male and female respondents were more likely to consider men as the “best” leader in this area.
- 22 percent of respondents felt that women should not participate in village meetings and decision-making with exactly the same qualifications.
- 58 percent considered that men were more likely to have the right skills and experience for parliament.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

As part of local governance, in addition to district and town officers, some villages have established councils to discuss priority issues and assist the district and town officers. The gender composition of these councils is not known.

The Community Development Plan (CDP) process has been instrumental in expanding women's participation in decision-making. The CDP identified village women’s practical priorities but women's strategic needs, such as the inclusion of women's concerns in village development committees, were not explicitly addressed by the CDP. Women are involved in the planning and implementation of local decision making related to local development. There are community development committees with female chairpersons in some villages. These committees have a number of subcommittees including women’s committees and subcommittees such as women's agriculture committees. More community awareness is required to ensure that women participate fully in the process. The exact nature and structure of these community development committees is not available.

There have been several programs – supported by development partners, regional networks, and also local civil society groups - to support women’s political participation. These include group work at the Fili'A Fafine Tonga (FFT) Women's Leadership Academy for young women and girls, which empowers female candidates;[1] and a post-election panel in December 2021 to discuss female candidates’ experiences of running for office.[2] The government has also signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the World Bank to support women’s political participation. The MoU includes a Memorandum of Understanding defining solidarity in supporting government commitments to gender equality, including women's political participation, in Tonga.

In terms of women in leadership roles, women make up 24 percent of Cabinet ministers and 80 percent of the total employees of Public Service Commission governed ministries.[3] In the private sector, 26 percent of board members are women. Women hold 15 percent of senior positions; women were found to hold 19 percent of Director, 17 percent of Deputy Chair, and 10 percent of Board Chair positions. Women were more highly represented as Directors on the boards of private sector organizations than in public service.

Tonga recognizes the need for enhancing women’s political participation and leadership. The National Women's Empowerment and Gender Equality Tonga (WEGET) Policy and Strategy Action Plan (2019- 2025) includes gender equality as one of its five priority areas, with a focus on increasing women’s representation in the five priority outcomes. The WEGET policy acknowledges the need for amending legislation and creating enabling environments to support women's political participation. The action plan includes various activities to increase women's representation in parliament and in elected local government offices, and increased participation of women in decision-making in all spheres. Some of these activities include providing training on gender equality and women’s rights; building women's leadership role visibility; providing capacity building support to women candidates and elected representatives, as well as building media’s capacity to gauge gender issues among others.[2]

Education

Though Tonga has high literacy rates at 99.4 percent, the overall performance of the country on education indicators need to improve.

The Government of Tonga recognizes the right of education of every child between the ages of 4 and 18 years. The Education Act (2013) and its revised version (2020) enforces the right of every child to receive quality education, and access to educational and vocational information and guidance.* The Act also provides for free primary school education, secondary education and vocational education and training (TVET) for girls and marginalized groups (including students with disabilities).

Percentage of government expenditure on education stands at 11.9 percent (2020), and roughly about 6.6 percent of the GDP.* Primary school attendance is close to universal. The Gross Enrollment Ratio (GER) was 108.5 for boys in 2020, while it was 107.3 for girls. This means that GER includes students who may be older or younger than the official age group). However, GER and completion rates are lower at secondary level.

The GER for secondary education was 95.6 percent for girls and 81.3 percent for boys in 2020. The lower secondary completion rate was 94.2 percent for girls and 70.7 percent for boys. About 64.8 percent of girls and 54.7 percent of boys completed upper secondary education, while only about 53.7 percent of girls and 51.3 percent of boys could demonstrate foundational numeracy skills.* In the second year, the 2019 Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey recorded a Net Attendance Rates of just 46 percent for girls in primary school (compared to 80.5 percent for boys), while only about 70.5 percent of girls were out of school at upper secondary level, with significantly higher rates among boys (41.1 percent) than girls (23.6 percent). The upper secondary completion rate was 44 percent (49 percent for girls and 38 percent for boys).

The table below shows Tonga's education sector performance on different party indices. A party index value less than 1 indicates disparity in favour of the advantaged group (i.e., boys, richest, urban), and a value higher than 1 indicates disparity in favour of disadvantaged group (i.e., girls, poorest, rural).

It is important to note that higher education for young women does not translate into better employment opportunities due to gender-based labour markets, perceptions about suitable occupations for men and women, and the expectation placed on women to engage in unpaid domestic and care work.

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Health, including sexual and reproductive health.

Compared to other countries in the Western Pacific region, Tonga has relatively advanced progress on key health indicators. The life expectancy at birth in Tonga was 75 years for men and 79 years for women in 2019. However, gaps exist in non-communicable diseases (NCDs) prevention and control. There is a high prevalence of NCDs such as diabetes and cardiovascular disease among women. Figure 2 shows sex disparities in the incidence of NCDs, with women accounting for 56% of all deaths due to mortality, 20.3 percent of female mortality and 29.5 percent of male mortality attributed to cardiovascular disease, cancer, diabetes, and chronic respiratory disease in 2019. 74 percent of the Tongan adult population was considered obese, and women (aged 25-69) and adults from higher quintiles were more likely to be obese.

On sexual and reproductive health, Tonga performs well in reproductive, maternal, newborn and child health (RMNCH), except for family planning and immunization. The total fertility rate (TFR) is 3.2, and the contraceptive prevalence rate (CPR) is 50%. However, only 41% of women aged 15-49 met their SDG indicator 3.2.1 on or under 5 mortality rates. The target is less than 25 deaths for every 1000 live births in the country by 2030. This means that 75% of women are not meeting this target, and the country faces significant challenges to meet maternal mortality rate targets (less than 70 per 100,000 live births) as well as other SDG indicators related to RMNCH.

The reasons for these are unclear; as skilled attendance at birth is high in urban areas, and 98 percent in rural areas. Institutional delivery is as high as 95 percent. A more detailed analysis is required to understand the situation fully.


Adolescent birth rate SDG 3.7.2 indicator is under target. By 2030, ensure universal access to sexual and reproductive health-care services, including for family planning, information and education, and the integration of reproductive health into national strategies and programmes. As of 2015,

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

### Adolescent birth rate is 30 per 1000 women aged 15-19. The proportion of women aged 20–24 giving birth within the last 12 months was 17 percent compared to national three percent.[1]

Abortion is illegal in Tonga. More importance needs to be given to reducing adolescent fertility and addressing the underlying causes of early pregnancy, including poverty, lack of education, and poor sexual well-being of adolescents. Preventing births very early in a woman’s life is an important measure to improve maternal health and reduce infant mortality.[2] 22 percent of women aged 15-49 had unmet needs for family planning.

Among 15–49-year-olds, 10.8 percent of women reported having a sexually transmitted infection (STI) or symptoms of an STI in the previous 12 months. Many sources on sexual and reproductive health and adolescent sexuality are not easily accessible to young people living in rural areas of Tonga. There is much cultural stigma and shame surrounding this. This stigma prevents the communication of all topics related to sexual and reproductive health, including contraception, safe sex, and access for disabled persons from acquiring knowledge about use and sexuality, as well as limiting access to services and information on these issues.

Climate change induced changes to rainfall patterns, and growing intensity of disasters in the country is not only affecting people’s lives and livelihoods but also impacting people's health. Tonga is experiencing more frequent and severe droughts, which are leading to water shortages. The quality of safe drinking water is also compromised due to various disasters, sea-level rise and saltwater intrusion, particularly in low-lying areas with shallow and thin groundwater levels. Vulnerability of health care facilities is increasing as a result of climate change. In the event of natural disasters such as cyclones affecting health care facilities, provision of health services may be affected in the face of sudden onset extreme weather events or other hazards. Women and vulnerable groups may be at risk of not receiving adequate health care in the future.

### Economic participation

The economy of Tonga is highly dependent on climate sensitive sectors such agriculture, fisheries and tourism which are a revenue base that is sensitive to external shocks. Pandemic and series of disasters led to contraction of economy by 2 percent in 2021; real GDP is estimated to have expanded by 3.7 percent in 2022.[3] The Tonga government’s economic growth rate for the period 2021–2025 is 3.7 percent. Tonga's public debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to rise and remain above the 70 percent debt ceiling set by the International Monetary Fund (IMF) in 2019, which will require increased spending needs over the long term to achieve its climate resilience and Sustainable Development Goals.[4]

The economy faces significant labour shortages due to labour participation in seasonal workers programs for tourism.

### The agriculture sector's share of GDP is 16.32 percent in 2021. The 2021 Household Income and Expenditure Survey shows that around 80 percent of households were participating in primary activities such as subsistence farming, fishing, or livestock rearing. Another 37 participated in household/home-produced food processing.[5] There are significant gender differences in the types of primary activities engaged by individuals. 98 percent of farmers and fishers were male; women reported doing handcrafts work, and whereas cropping is exclusively undertaken by males.[6]

Access to resources
- 90 percent of the households in Tonga receive remittance, which accounts for 37.7 percent of GDP.
- Around 60 percent of household income comes from work, meaning cash from employer or business (42.5 percent) and subsistence from the latter activities (18.2 percent).
- Personal inter-societal gifts (and remittances) represent important source of income (30 percent) as do the shares of cash gifts received or remittance received (27.9 percent).

Income sources vary considerably across locations. Income from employment comprises a much higher share in Tongatapu (42.5 percent and 34.6 percent respectively in urban and rural areas) while it represents only 18.2 percent of income in outlying islands. The main sources are rural activities (agriculture, fishing, livestock and handicraft) which provide with cash money as well as means of subsistence. Cash gifts received or remittance received and subsistence from these activities account for 45 percent to 45 percent of income in outer islands.

The above context demonstrates the importance of access to land to engage income generating activities (agriculture, fishing, livestock and handicraft). Women are not guaranteed by tradition and by law, land rights are guaranteed only to men. Tongan men are guaranteed their rights to an ipu (aloa
