# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Digital connectivity

Digital connectivity is critical aspect of modern life — as it opens up access to socio-economic opportunities. In Tonga, 53 percent of women and 47 percent of men own a mobile phone, and 53.8 percent of women and 60.2 percent of men have internet access at home. However, Tonga’s low levels of digital connectivity are reflected in the country’s low levels of economic development. Tonga’s target of universal internet access by 2025. Tonga also lacks sex or gender disaggregated data and gender equality is not a priority for Tonga’s national statistical system. The government’s efforts with gender relations, and cultural transformations, are underway. The country also wants to translate media into local languages. In addition, the government of Tonga has launched a National Strategy on Gender Equality and Empowerment, including energy, and climate change.

## Women with Disabilities

Percentage of the population with disability is 6.4 percent (male 5.7 percent; female 7 percent). The prevalence rate for data in Tonga is sparse. The 2018 Tonga Disability Survey shows that disability prevalence is 2.2 percent among children aged 4–2, 2.4 percent among children aged 5–12 and 11.4 percent among adults aged 13+. In the 2017 Tonga Census of Population and Housing rural recorded the highest prevalence rate of 47.1 percent; Tongatapu urban at 21.3 percent; Vavau at 18.4 percent, Ha'apai at 9.5 percent; Eua at 6.4 percent and Tonga Niu at 0.7 percent. A substantial proportion of people with disabilities are living in poverty. The prevalence rate for disability is high among women aged 20–39 years old. People with disability are one of the most marginalized and vulnerable groups in Tongan society, often facing stigma, exclusion and discrimination, and lack voice and influence over community and national resources.

Households with persons with disability had higher average and median expenditure than households without persons with disability. In terms of access to education, 94 percent of persons with disabilities have ever attended school compared to 100 percent of persons without disabilities. Among working age, 91 percent of people without disability work in a mobile phone activity versus 75 percent for persons with a disability. Related to livelihood, 59 percent of people with disability undertake handicrafts as their main economic activity and 42 percent sell goods at markets or shops compared to 68 percent of those most prevalent activity, compared to 42 percent of females, 32 percent of all the population aged 15+ years.

It is likely that persons with disabilities are more engaged in households/family duties due to lack of access to other spaces. A high proportion of people with disability, notably women, find it very difficult or not at all easy to make public places accessible to them. This includes the need for ramps and other needs to be done to make public places, facilities for community events like voting and commercial precincts accessible to people with disabilities.

The 2018 survey also notes a higher proportion of women without disabilities able to participate in Government decision-making (64 percent compared to 68.4 for those with disabilities, indicating that Government decision making could be more inclusive. There are no significant differences between men and women with disabilities on this issue. However, it is interesting to note that both groups find it difficult to participate in household decision making than women with no functional disability.

Given the above context, the project recognizes the need for ensuring participation of people (especially women with disabilities) in setting the transformative adaptation agenda and in coastal resilience activities.


## Gender Based Violence

### Gender Based Violence (GBV) is a major issue across the country. The 2019 Multi-indicator Cluster Survey (MICS) provides data on some forms of GBV, including domestic violence.

Data show that women than men justly domestic violence. About 3 in 5 women and 1 in 5 men aged 15-49 years justified husbands beating their wives for specific reasons, such as, going out without telling husband; neglecting children; arguing with husband; refuses sex with husband or burns the food (20 percent).

Close to 3 in 10 ever married women aged 15–49 years has had lifetime experienced emotional, physical and sexual violence. About one-third of these women have experienced at least once in the last 12 months preceding the 2019 MICS survey. One-in-ten women aged 15–49 years have ever experienced sexual violence (including rape) during their lifetime according to the 2019 MICS survey). Of those women aged 15–49 years who have experienced any physical or sexual violence, only 13 percent received help to stop violence, and about 7 percent sought help to tell anyone about the violence faced.

1 percent of the women aged 15-49 that indicated that experienced non-partner sexual violence in the 12 months preceding the 2019 survey, and 3 percent indicated having experienced non-partner sexual violence in their lifetime (MOH 2.2.1). In Tonga found that women in Tonga experience violence by non-partners (especially fathers and teachers) three times more often than boys.

The non-partner and intimate partner violence at home could be an explanation for 22 percent of women in rural areas reporting to have experienced sexual violence, compared with only 10.5 percent of men.

The impact of violence on health, socio-economic development of women is very high as it prevents them from participating in the economy. The Government of Tonga, with support from donors, civil society partners, NGOs/NGOs as well as faith-based organizations are responding to address GBV and other forms of violence against women and children. Women's Coalition Against Child Abuse (WCCA), founded in 2005, provides counseling services, refuge and advocacy for women survivors/victims. The Family Protection Act 2013 (FPA) provides the legal framework to seek protection from domestic violence and sexual harassment, with financial resources for implementation of the FPA and No Drop policy by the police.

## Gender, Climate Change and Disaster Risk

Tonga's Climate Change Policy recognizes that different impacts of climate change on men and women. article 170 of the Constitution of Tonga (2013) with the WIGET project, that considerations of gender, disability and vulnerability must be placed at the center of all planning and climate change disaster preparedness and response strategies. The Government of Tonga is a signatory to the Beijing Platform for Action and the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). It has also signed the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, which calls for greater gender equality and women's inclusion (GEI) for resilient development.

Global women and children are 14 times more likely to die from disasters than men. In the 2009 Tonga cyclone, 85 percent of those who died were women and girls. In 2012 triggered by the Hunga-Tonga-Hunga Haapai volcanic eruption, of the four fatalities, 3 were women (in other words 75 percent of the fatalities were women). 80 percent of the population was affected by tropical cyclone Gita (2019) and 64.5 percent of those who died were women and girls.

Women are more vulnerable to disasters because they have less access to information, in accessing relief measures, significantly more women (7214) than men (1430) accessed shelter relief and non-food items. This data can be interpreted in different ways – a) that more women are

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Vulnerable groups

Women are more vulnerable than men in Tonga due to traditional cultural attitudes that prevent women from accessing relief services.

### Economic vulnerability

- Women face increased risk of GBV post-disaster.
- Women participate in smaller numbers in formal and informal employment, which limits their ability to earn a living.
- Women are less likely to receive critical information on emergency preparedness or to participate in disaster response activities due to cultural norms.

### Disaster Preparedness

- Disaster preparedness committees are largely made up of men who lead discussions that may not address the specific needs of women and people with disabilities.

## Response and Preparedness measures

### Vulnerability of elderly, youth, and people with disabilities

These groups face increased vulnerability in Tonga due to climate change and disasters. Special measures are required to reduce their vulnerability.

### Gender and coastal resilience (with a focus on Hahake)

The Hahake peninsula area is particularly vulnerable to SLR and flooding. Coastal protection works will be constructed along the shoreline, but there may be impacts on livelihoods in the freshwater swamp land which is used for taro and tree crops.

## Gender and decision-making

### Inclusion and participation

- Women's inclusion and participation are crucial in decision-making processes related to coastal resilience.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

processes in Tonga that promote community-based resilience are the CDNs and district plans, and the Special Management Areas (SMA). While the CDP process has provided an avenue for women’s participation in decision-making processes, there remains limited (or no political participation). Added to this, CDs (and by extension district plans) are also limited in scope in incorporating climate risks and promoting long-term adaptation planning. The lack of a gender analysis in these documents is a significant oversight which prevents an informed engagement on long-term risks and potential solutions.

Enhancing women’s access to resources and increasing their participation in decision-making for women to develop local climate solutions is necessary for enhancing women’s climate change resilience.

Kolonga Nukulaele are also declared Special Management Areas (SMAs). SMAs are part of a community-based fishery programme in Tonga, where designated communities are granted legal rights to manage their coastal fish resources. Within each SMA is at least one fish habitat that provides protection for the breeding and nursery grounds of many species of marine species, no-one including the community are allowed to fish or harvest from the FHR.

The SMA programme is gender-responsive and promotes inclusion of women in the SMA management committees. Women’s participation varies across SMAs, with a majority of both men (60 percent) and women (40 percent). However, gaps remain in full engagement of women in decision-making processes. The survey on women’s role in fisheries management support for women knowledge and engagement in fisheries management – more men than women found the inclusion and recognition of women’s roles as being supported by SMA-management, decisions made by SMA-management, and the overall SMA programme. Equal engagement of women in SMA has occurred compared to men. Women accounted for only 35 percent of SMA members, with 60 percent of SMA members being male.

The insights from the findings from the survey on the SMA programme on women’s engagement in decision-making processes are that more needs to be done to ensure full and equal analysis is required to identify the different roles of men and women in the SMA management and how meaningful engagement of women can be promoted to enhance coastal resilience. Beyond this, there is a need for a gender analysis as part of the long-term adaptation planning that takes a critical look at the various risks and solutions are not present. Even the National Infrastructure Investment Plan (2021-2030) does not elaborate on enhancing women’s access to resources and increasing their participation in decision-making (beyond infrastructure development), beyond initial community consultation. A core priority project identified in the infrastructure investment plan was to build a women’s centre for handicraft processing.

* **Livelihood:** As indicated in the survey, economic participation, and access to resources, women’s livelihoods are lower than men’s. Women have less control over natural resources; more men than women are engaged in reef fishing and other types of fishing and gathering for shells and invertebrates for household consumption. Women are mostly engaged in harvesting sea grass and seaweed for sale or for household consumption. The survey also indicated that women Tongatapu island have more opportunities to participate in the formal economy (see section on economic participation, ownership, control). This can provide vital raw materials for household consumption and for manufacturing of traditional goods.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

Additionally, as mentioned above, the freshwater swamp on the landside section of the Kolonga section is a key agricultural area and a drinking water source. Protecting the swamp through conservation measures such as the continued use of traditional land tenure systems in that area, at least short-term land tenancy adaptation measures can help to reduce vulnerability.

## Disaster risk reduction: Women are disproportionately affected by disasters. In Tonga, though some are more vulnerable than others, women and girls are generally considered to be most likely to be affected more which reduces their resilience to disaster risks. Gender-based violence is prevalent in Tonga and women are particularly vulnerable during natural disasters and other health issues because of disasters.

### Infrastructure and road damage, especially in rural areas, impacts women more by limiting their mobility and increasing their vulnerability.

The swamp provides a vital water source for the Kolonga village and serves as an important evacuation route and will help in reducing women’s vulnerability during disaster. III. Mechanisms to promote gender equality in Tonga – Legal and administrative frameworks

Tonga is not one of the signatory countries to the Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of discrimination against Women (CEDAW), as that would require amending the land ownership system, which remains a key issue for women’s rights in Tonga. However, Tonga has made a number of international and regional gender equality conventions. These include the following:

- Beijing Platform for Action of Women (Sep 1995);
- Millennium Declaration (2000);
- Commonwealth Plan of Action for Gender Equality (1995);
- United Nations Programme of Action on Population and Development (UNPAPD) (1994), on the Implementation of the Outcomes of the World Summit for Social Development (May 2000); and on the Implementation of the Outcomes of the International Conference on Population and Development (ICPD) (1994);
- Declaration and Program of Action of the UN World Summit for Social Development, Copenhagen Declaration (1995);
- UN International Conference on Population and Development;
- present Constitution;
- The Pacific Platform for Action on Women and Development (1994);
- The Pacific Plan and various Pacific Islands Forum Summits Communiques; and
- Pacific Community’s Gender Equality Strategy (2013-2018).

Despite existence of some laws discriminate against women, notably those related to land ownership and the right to vote, Tonga has made significant progress in promoting gender equality. Tonga pledged its commitment to the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including achieving gender equality by 2030.

Nationally, the Tonga Strategic Development Framework IIk (2015-2025) aims to promote a more progressive Tonga supporting a higher quality of life for all through inclusive and sustainable growth and development. The SDG framework has five strategic outcomes, and 29 organizational outcomes grouped into five pillars. National Outcome 3 focuses on “inclusive, sustainable and empowering human development with gender equality”.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

The revised National Women’s Empowerment and Gender Equality Tonga (WEGET) Policy and Strategic Plan of Action 2019–2025 overall objective is to support achievement of the national outcome.

3. The policy has five priority outcomes:

- **Ensure women’s empowerment mainstreaming** across government policies, programmes and services, corporate budgeting and monitoring and evaluation
- **Families and communities prosper from gender equality**
- **Equitable access to economic assets and employment**
- **Increased women’s leadership and equitable political representation**
- **Create equal-conditions to respond to natural disasters, environmental challenges and climate change**

People with disabilities and vulnerable groups are integral to the 5 (priority) outcomes.

The Women's Affairs and Gender Equality Division (WAGED) within the Ministry of Internal Affairs has the overall mandate for implementing WEGET policy and supporting other line ministries to mainstream gender equality. The WAGED developed a Gender Mainstreaming Handbook (2019), and a status report on gender equality in Tonga titled, *Gender Equality Status Report 2018*. The handbook provides guidance on how to implement the policy and identifies 6 entry points for mainstreaming gender. Additional capacity development support may be required for different ministries and departments to fully utilize the handbook and mainstream gender.

In the 2019 UNDP Gender Assessment, Tonga was identified as a country with high potential for implementation of Sustainable Development Goals, recognized gender as a cross-cutting issue and the role of women in climate change adaptation. The assessment also highlighted that there is strong buy-in with MIDECC role in mainstreaming climate change. The audit report called for performance audit of gender equality initiatives.

However, with COVID19, implementation of the above initiatives had stalled. In addition, the WEGET policy also identifies a weak enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in the country. WEGET policy refers to the 2018 UNDP Gender Assessment and the 2017 Ministry of Internal Affairs (MOIA), which found that only 4 percent of the projects assessed had targeted action to advance gender equality, and 37 percent of projects assessed mainstreamed gender and have a component on gender analysis in project design. The assessment also noted that 58 percent of the projects were led by women (44 percent were gender blind, and 21 percent projects had limited gender elements).

The assessment found that departments and agencies involved in infrastructure or technical activities were significantly more likely to mainstream gender than those involved in social services or health, than those by ministries involved in the provision of human services. Infrastructure projects are not usually planned with a focus on women’s needs and priorities. The lack of attention to how local community and social dynamics inadvertently or indirectly, sometimes significantly – a key finding for this project implementation. This observation demonstrates the need to build gender and social inclusion into infrastructure development planning and design. It is also important that there be more appropriate and accessible technical expertise to call upon. Inability to mainstream gender could affect Tonga’s ability to respond effectively to climate change and natural disasters, among other things, not to ensure that vulnerable groups are adequately supported to prepare for, survive and recover from the impacts of disasters and climate change.

[1] UNDP (2018), Tonga Climate Finance and Risk Governance Assessment. [https://www.un.org/en/sections/development-cooperation/gender-climate-change-and-disaster-risk-management/](https://www.un.org/en/sections/development-cooperation/gender-climate-change-and-disaster-risk-management/)
[2] Ibid

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

Other factors that have impacted the weak enabling environment for gender mainstreaming included constant changes to the accountable ministers and delays in recruitment of CEOs. Additionally, lack of gender budgeting and a lack of strategic plans for gender equality, resulting from WFP’s lack of institutional data collection capacity (especially disaggregated data by gender) and public sector stakeholder awareness and capacity to mainstream gender impacts the overall ability of the government to empower women.

Despite these challenges, the government is committed to promoting gender equality and there is buy-in from key ministries to mainstream gender. Specifically, The Joint National Action Plan on Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction (JNAP) includes gender equality and vulnerability considerations in all planning, and climate change, disaster preparedness and response activities. JNAP-2 target 17 is on achieving gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) for resilient development.

## IV. Gender Analysis and Recommendations

The gender assessment and analysis undertaken during the design of this project led to including gender considerations across project outputs and activities and will serve as the basis for gender-responsive monitoring, evaluation, learning and reporting (MER). The MER framework will include updates and revision throughout the lifetime of the project to ensure that project does not negatively affect local communities or contribute to climate change. It also considers resilience of local communities, including the marginalized and socially vulnerable groups.

During the project design phase, consultations were held with a diverse range of stakeholders and partners, including: Tonga’s Ministry of Women’s Affairs (MoWA), Ministry of Education, Training and Youth Development (METYD), Ministry of Lands and Natural Resources (MLNR), Prime Minister's office (Department of Local Government), National Statistics Office (NSO), National Environment Management Authority (NEMA) and other development partners such as ADB, EU, SPPED and NGOs such as the Civil Society for Tonga (CST), the Mainstreaming Rural Development Innovation (MORDI), and the Vava’u Environmental Protection Association (VEPA). The project also aligns with the National Strategic Plan on Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction (NSPCCDRR) 2019-2035, the Tonga Multi Indicator Cluster Survey, 2018 National Disability Survey, 2017 National Health Survey, 2016 National Population Census, the Sustainable Development Framework II (SDFII), Joint National Action Plan on Climate Change and Disaster Risk (JNAP-2018), and National Strategic Plan for Women’s Empowerment.

The gender analysis, through stakeholder engagement and consultations, enabled the following:

* Acknowledgement of the need for expanding public spaces for equitable engagement of men, women, youth and other vulnerable groups.
* Identification of project activities that reinforce women’s, men’s and youth’s empowerment in social, institutional, and economic landscape particularly in the context of coastal vulnerability and climate change.
* Initial awareness raising among women’s groups, youth groups, church groups and other vulnerable groups on gender equality issues.
* Elicting women’s and other vulnerable group concerns about coastal erosion and flood related risks; and
* Identifying specific roles and responsibilities that can be played by women and other vulnerable groups during the project implementation and verifying their commitment for their support.
* Ensuring that gender analysis (differentiated impact on men and women) is integrated into all capacity development activities.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Ensuring the grievances mechanism is accessible to all groups of society.

This gender assessment and stakeholder consultations have identified the following areas as particularly important dimensions to be reflected in the implementation strategy of the project:

### Gender Analysis
- The different needs in responding to extreme weather events faced by women and men, as well as youth.
- Analysis of the gender-based division of labour which can be reflected in the engagement of women, men and youth in project activities;
- Reflection on how women, men and youth access environmental resources and the goods and services that they provide into the design of ecosystem restoration and monitoring activities;
- The need to collect age and disaggregated data during the project implementation so that gender- and age-responsive decisions can be supported.

### Voice and participation
- Ensure equitable participation of women, men, youth and elderly people at community-level adaptation planning.
- Create opportunities for women to voice their views on transformative agenda for adaptation, and in the development and implementation of climate and risk informed Community Development Plans (CDP) (component 1).
- Ensure the participation of women’s groups, youth groups and church groups in community mobilization activities;
- Create opportunities for participation of vulnerable and people with disabilities in all project consultations and planning processes.
- Adjusting advocacy and awareness raising approach to different population groups; for sensitive topics, women-only, men-only, or youth-only meetings may be needed and organized separately from the general public meeting to ensure their meaningful participation;
- Include women involved in the project to develop awareness raising / training aimed at drawing attention to the implication of climate resilience adaptation and gender equality;
- Understand the barriers and challenges and dialogue in relation to gender and social inclusion in climate and disaster resilience.
- Organize design workshops (targeting women and people with disabilities) to develop transformative solutions.

## V. Implementation arrangements for the Gender Action Plan

UNDP – as the Accredited Entity - will be responsible for the overall implementation of the Gender Action Plan, working in close collaboration with the respective entities – the Department of Climate Change under the Ministry of Meteorology, Energy, Information, Disaster Management, Environment, Climate Change and Meteorology (MEMDEC), the Ministry of Lands and Natural Resources (MLNR) and Ministry of Infrastructure (MoI).

Ministry of Internal Affairs (MIA) which is also part of the Joint National Action Plan for Climate Change - 2 or INAP – (2018-2028) Task Force is indeed a key partner in the implementation of the project, as well as the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade. The MIA will provide coordination support for mainstreaming gender across all three output areas.

The Project Management Unit (PMU) for the project will be based in Tonga and an International Gender Specialist will be part of the PMU). Gender technical expertise and assistance (including from civil society organizations) will be sought to supplement the available skill set and experience of the PMU. In addition, a gender focal person will be appointed within each implementing partner organization and highlighted in the GAP below.

The GAP also acknowledged existing gender capacity gaps in the country. Though gender inclusivity is one of the 22 targets of JNAP-2, and Gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) for resilient development is one of the 22 targets of JNAP-2, MEIDECC serves as the secretariat for the JNAP-2 and the GESI target. The Women’s Affairs Division does not have the technical expertise on gender and climate change and enhancing resilience. Therefore, as part of the implementation of the GAP, the PMU (especially the Gender Specialist) will provide training to the WAD staff on the GAP and organize capacity development training on gender responsive climate resilience.

*Note on activity 1.3.3. Given the high number of women officials in the government of Tonga rather than indicating 30 or 50 percent of participants should be women, the focus should on the participation of relevant decision makers in capacity development trainings to strengthen their skills and knowledge.

**Note on activity 2.1.1. Use of LiDAR and satellite imagery is technical and there is no scope for mainstreaming gender. However, in the analysis of potential impact of climate change risks, it is important to mainstream gender to understand gender specific impacts.
